However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Jolly, E., & Chang, L. J. (2021). Gossip drives vicarious learning and facilitates social connection. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing private communication (gossip) in a repeated public goods game increases average group contributions and slows the decline of cooperation over rounds, compared to no-communication conditions. Gossip is especially important when direct observation of others' behavior is limited, as it enables vicarious learning and helps participants adjust their own contributions based on information received from others. Gossip also increases social bonding and alignment of impressions between conversation partners. The study does not include any formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (gossip) in public goods games increases contribution rates and sustains cooperation, especially under incomplete information. However, it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that social information transmission (gossip) can substitute for formal sanctions in sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes or for games with punishment enabled. The design dimensions most directly addressed are player count, number of rounds, chat/communication, and information visibility. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, W. J., Li, X., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Social exclusion with dynamic cost on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing reputation-dependent imitation and a strict reputation evaluation mechanism (low tolerance for defection) in a spatial public goods game leads to higher and more stable levels of cooperation (as measured by the frequency of cooperators). The effect is stronger when the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is higher. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings about reputation-dependent imitation and tolerance in reputation evaluation may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a punishment mechanism limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Stoop, J., van Soest, D., & Vyrastekova, J. (2018). Rewards and cooperation in social dilemma games. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that net-positive rewards (where the recipient gains more than the sender loses) dramatically increase cooperation and efficiency in standard public goods games (VCM), with most groups reaching high contribution levels when rewards are available. In contrast, in the Claim game—where players can undo others' contributions by taking from the public pool—rewards have only a marginal effect on cooperation, and average net contributions are not significantly different from zero. In the Claim game, rewards are often exchanged bilaterally and are not strongly linked to cooperative behavior in the social dilemma. The results suggest that the effectiveness of rewards in promoting efficiency depends critically on the structure of the social dilemma, particularly whether others can undo cooperative actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends on whether the game allows for negative actions (i.e., taking from the public pool) and whether the MPCR is certain. In standard VCMs (linear PGGs), rewards can substantially increase efficiency, but in environments where contributions can be undone (as in the Claim game or CPR games), rewards are much less effective. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'take' option or uncertain MPCR is a critical moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency with rewards if the game allows for undoing others' contributions. The findings do not inform about punishment effects, as punishment is not studied.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Yang, R. (2019). Using rewards reasonably: The effects of stratified-rewards in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stratified-rewards can promote, maintain, or even inhibit efficiency (measured as available funds for public goods) depending on the scope and distribution of rewards. If the number of people who receive rewards is small and the number who get high-level rewards is controlled, available funds can increase. If too many people get high rewards, available funds can fall below the baseline, even if all players cooperate. The sum of contributions and the standard deviation of contributions are also affected by reward parameters. The fraction of cooperators is always high with rewards, but this does not guarantee high efficiency. The optimal reward design balances the number of recipients and the cost of rewards to maximize available funds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of reward mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like environments is highly sensitive to the design of the reward scheme. It shows that simply enabling rewards does not guarantee higher efficiency; the number of rewarded players, the proportion of high-level rewards, and the cost of rewards all interact to determine the net group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a reward mechanism can have positive, neutral, or negative effects on efficiency, depending on these design details. Since the paper does not study punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment, but it does highlight the importance of cost structures and incentive distribution in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Schmidt, K. M., & Ockenfels, A. (2021). Focusing climate negotiations on a uniform common commitment can promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negotiation design is crucial for promoting cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. Uniform common commitments (UCC) result in group investments averaging 83% of the social optimum with enforcement, compared to 45% (individual commitments, IC) and 48% (complex common commitments, CCC). Even without enforcement, UCC achieves 52% of the optimum, while IC and CCC achieve only 34%. The UCC design is robust to asymmetries and to the absence of enforcement. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that negotiation design—specifically, requiring a uniform minimum contribution—can dramatically increase group efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of enforcement. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding the impact of commitment and negotiation structure, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Ren, T. Y., & Zheng, J. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics in the spatial public goods game with tolerance-based expulsion and cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with four players and 20 rounds per part, groups that start with unequal endowments (two rich, two poor) and then experience redistribution to equality do not increase their group contributions or efficiency after redistribution. Group contributions in the post-redistribution phase are indistinguishable from those in groups that remain unequal, and both are significantly lower than in always-equal groups. The experience of initial inequality creates a persistent negative spillover, locking groups into low cooperation and efficiency even after equality is restored. The mechanism is traced to the former poor mimicking the low-contribution behavior of the former rich after redistribution. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, simply reducing inequality via redistribution does not increase efficiency in public goods games if groups have previously experienced inequality. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, for games with similar design (no punishment, no reward, linear PGG, 4 players, 20+20 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no identification, no summary of individual contributions), the efficiency in the treatment with redistribution will not exceed that of the control with persistent inequality, and both will be lower than in always-equal groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGGs without punishment or reward, but do not inform about the effect of punishment.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Cooperation and competition between pair and multi-player social games in spatial populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary competition between 'alone' (pairwise) and 'together' (group) attitudes leads to complex phase behavior. At low public goods multiplication factors, pairwise interactions dominate; at intermediate values, both attitudes coexist but this can paradoxically lower average income; at high multiplication factors, group interactions dominate and cooperation can be sustained. The presence of the 'together' attitude at low multiplication factors can actually reduce average income, contrary to intuition. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding how the structure of interaction (pairwise vs. group) and the public goods multiplication factor affect efficiency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Isler, O., Maule, J., & Starmer, C. (2018). Is intuition really cooperative? Improved tests support the social heuristics hypothesis. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time-pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases contributions to the public good compared to forced-delay (reflective decision-making), especially under certain instructional conditions. The effect is robust to compliance and understanding confounds. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment condition or analyze payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on contribution rates under different cognitive conditions may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, no punishment, no reward, no chat) may help contextualize the baseline, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: van den Berg, P., Dewitte, P., Aertgeerts, I., & Wenseleers, T. (2020). How the incentive to contribute affects contributions in the one-shot public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions in a one-shot public goods game increase with the marginal per capita return (MPCR), with most of the increase occurring at lower MPCR values (below 0.58–0.64). At higher MPCR, contributions plateau well below full cooperation. The experiment uses groups of three, no punishment or reward, and no communication. The findings provide a high-resolution mapping of how MPCR shapes contribution behavior, but do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in public goods games as a function of MPCR, group size, and the absence of punishment or reward. It does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency (without punishment) given MPCR and group size, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Yam, P. P. C., Ng, G. T. T., Au, W. T., Tao, L., Lu, S., Leung, H., & Fung, J. M. Y. (2018). The effect of subgroup homogeneity of efficacy on contribution in public good dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that arranging participants into homogeneous or heterogeneous subgroups of efficacy does not significantly affect the number or proportion of contributions to the public good. This is because two mediating mechanisms—perceived efficacy and expectation of subgroup contribution—operate in opposite directions and cancel each other out. The experiment does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are limited to contribution behavior and psychological mediators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed. The design dimensions studied (subgroup homogeneity, efficacy) are not among the core prediction dimensions for punishment effects. The outcome measures are contribution rates and psychological variables, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Battu, B., & Srinivasan, N. (2020). Evolution of conditional cooperation in public good games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that high and stable cooperation (measured by donation rates) can be achieved in repeated public good games when agents' social learning is sufficiently biased toward prestige (social value) or when both prestige and payoff are weighted but with some noise. When imitation is driven mainly by payoff, cooperation collapses. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how social learning mechanisms (prestige vs. payoff) affect the evolution of cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that cooperation can be sustained by prestige-biased social learning even in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable quantitative or qualitative evidence about the efficiency impact of punishment or about moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Yang, H. X., Guo, W. Z., & Liu, G. G. (2018). Promotion of cooperation based on swarm intelligence in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of swarm intelligence (where players update their strategies based on both their own best past strategy and the best current strategy in their neighborhood) into a continuous spatial public goods game leads to a strong promotion of cooperation. The level of cooperation depends on the multiplication factor (r) and the weight given to personal vs. neighborhood best strategies (c1). For low c1 (neighborhood-focused), cooperation can dominate at high r, but is vulnerable at low r. For high c1 (personal best-focused), cooperation is more stable even at low r, but the system is more diverse in strategies. The main reported outcome is the average cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how alternative strategy updating rules (swarm intelligence) affect cooperation rates, not payoffs or efficiency. It does, however, confirm that cooperation can be promoted by non-punitive mechanisms, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

The mechanism leads to the formation of larger and more robust cooperation clusters, which can dominate the population under favorable conditions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Freedom to choose between public resources promotes cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, M., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2019). Dynamic scale return coefficient with environmental feedback promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of a dynamic scale return coefficient, governed by the difference between a group's average reputation and the population average, promotes the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Higher reputation amplitude (the parameter controlling the impact of reputation differences) lowers the threshold for the enhancement factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the stable level of cooperation. The effect is robust across lattice and small-world network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

It is focused on a different cooperation-promoting mechanism (reputation-based environmental feedback) and does not include or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Quan, J., Zhou, Y. W., Zhang, M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2019). The impact of heterogeneous scale return coefficient between groups on the emergence of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient between groups (i.e., making the returns to investment nonlinear and group-specific) generally increases the average cooperation level compared to a homogeneous (linear) setting. The effect depends on the type of heterogeneity (uniform, exponential, power-law) and its amplitude. Uniform heterogeneity is most conducive to high and stable cooperation in most parameter ranges, but exponential heterogeneity can outperform in some cases. Stronger heterogeneity (higher amplitude) generally promotes higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency-related outcomes. Its findings are limited to how heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient affects average cooperation (contribution) levels in a spatial, continuous-contribution PGG. It is not relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be contextually useful for understanding how nonlinearities and heterogeneity in returns affect cooperation rates.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Pan, Q. H., Ju, X. X., & He, M. F. (2021). Public goods game with the interdependence of different cooperative strategies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on coevolving networks where agents disconnect from neighbors with similar payoff and connect to those with different payoff, the maintenance of cooperation depends on the balance between these two tendencies. Cooperation is best sustained when the rates of disconnection and connection are moderate and well-matched. Too much network time-dependence (frequent rewiring) generally undermines cooperation compared to the classic static network case. The emergence and stability of cooperation are associated with changes in network cohesion, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network coevolution (rewiring based on payoff similarity/difference) affects cooperation rates, not efficiency. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in investment, where individuals allocate more to groups that have yielded higher payoffs in the recent past, significantly increases the fraction of cooperators in spatial PGGs. Shorter memory (small M) and lower weight on earlier payoffs (small or negative omega) further enhance cooperation. Stronger heterogeneity (higher alpha) also promotes cooperation. These effects are robust to changes in network topology (square lattice vs. ER random networks). However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only adjacent, not direct, evidence.

- source: Huang, C. W., Han, W. C., Li, H. H., Cheng, H. Y., Dai, Q. L., & Yang, J. Z. (2019). Public cooperation in two-layer networks with asymmetric interaction and learning environments. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the structure of the interaction and learning networks (specifically, their overlap) can significantly affect the steady-state level of cooperation in a spatial PGG. For low synergy factor r, higher overlap between interaction and learning networks increases the fraction of cooperators; for high r, lower overlap increases cooperation. The results are robust to changes in network topology and node degree. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.

- source: Wu, Z. H., Huang, H., & Liao, Q. H. (2021). The study on the role of dedicators on promoting cooperation in public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The presence of dedicators in a PGG model can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation, leading to higher total contributions. Dedicators' willingness to donate is more important than the amount they donate. Emotional effects—whereby others are inspired to cooperate by dedicator actions—further enhance cooperation. The system is robust to several parameter changes, but a too-high budget requirement can collapse cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled or analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (such as the presence of prosocial 'dedicators' and emotional contagion) can promote cooperation and higher contributions. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the required sense.

No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.

There is no reliable generalizability of results, and evidence for parallelism between game and real-world behavior is mixed. The review highlights the importance of context and framing in interpreting game results and cautions against assuming that game behavior reflects real-world preferences or norms. The paper does not address punishment or efficiency outcomes directly.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The roles of particle swarm intelligence in the prisoner's dilemma based on continuous and mixed strategy systems on scale-free networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The authors propose a method to predict the strategies of players whose current actions are hidden, based on the similarity of their past behavior to that of observed players. The method is tested on both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods games on a spatial lattice. The prediction method achieves high accuracy (success rate up to 0.9) in identifying hidden strategies, with performance depending on the length of historical data and game parameters (temptation to defect in PDG, enhancement factor in PGG). The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.

It does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. - source: Heine, F., & Strobel, M. (2020). Reward and punishment in a team contest. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares reward and punishment mechanisms in a team contest game (with and without between-group competition). Groups of four play 15 rounds, deciding how much to contribute to a group lottery (contest) and then can reward or punish teammates at a cost. The main findings are: (1) Both punishment and reward mechanisms lead to over-contribution relative to Nash equilibrium, causing significant efficiency losses (rent dissipation). (2) Punishment does not increase efficiency; it reduces it, as punishing is costly and does not significantly increase contributions. (3) Rewarding increases contributions more than punishment, but is welfare-neutral (cost to giver equals benefit to receiver). (4) The contest environment (with an opponent group) leads to even greater inefficiency and lower average payoffs than the non-contest environment. (5) The R&P treatment (both reward and punishment available) in the contest environment is the least efficient, with the highest overspending and lowest mean income. (6) Receiving reward increases subsequent contributions, but punishment does not. (7) Overall, the presence of punishment does not solve the efficiency problem in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team contest environment with public-good-like features, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it, especially when both reward and punishment are available. The effect of punishment is less positive (more negative) on efficiency compared to reward or baseline. The contest structure (between-group competition) amplifies inefficiency. For prediction, if the control game (no punishment) is already inefficient due to over-contribution, enabling punishment is likely to further reduce efficiency, not improve it. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR, punishment cost and tech, no identification of punishers, no communication) are all specified and match standard lab contest/PGG designs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled in similar environments.

- source: Wegmann, J., & Musshoff, O. (2019). Groundwater management institutions in the face of rapid urbanization - Results of a framed field experiment in Bengaluru, India. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both externally imposed punishment and reward rules, implemented via random monitoring and sanctions/rewards for exceeding/following optimal extraction, significantly reduce over-extraction of groundwater in a framed field experiment with 600 real-world users. Social efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) increased from 86% in the control to 97% under punishment and 95% under reward, with welfare gains of 8% and 6% over control, respectively (both statistically significant at 1%). Communication alone had a much smaller effect (90% efficiency, 1% gain, not significant). The effect of punishment and reward was not significantly different between rural and urban areas. User types (myopic, rational, social optimal) persisted across treatments, but all treatments crowded out the most pro-social (social optimal) users to some extent. The main outcome is that externally imposed monitoring and sanctioning (punishment) is highly effective at increasing efficiency in this CPR context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with real-world users, adding an externally imposed punishment mechanism (random monitoring, monetary penalty for over-extraction) increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) by a substantial and statistically significant margin compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is robust to user type and urbanization. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close CPR variant with continuous extraction, group interdependence, and profit-based payoffs. The punishment cost and technology are specified, and the efficiency gains are quantified. This evidence should increase predicted efficiency for similar games when punishment is enabled, especially when the control efficiency is below the social optimum.

- source: Lohse, J., & Waichman, I. (2020). The effects of contemporaneous peer punishment on cooperation with the future. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a multigenerational public goods game, voluntary cooperation is difficult to sustain across generations. When peer punishment is available within a generation, the likelihood that the group meets the contribution threshold and sustains future generations more than doubles compared to the baseline. However, the increase in group earnings from more sustained generations is offset by the costs incurred through punishment, resulting in no significant difference in average aggregate earnings (efficiency) between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The effect of punishment on individual contribution levels is small, but it is sufficient to increase the rate at which groups meet the threshold. Punishment is mainly directed at those who contribute less than their peers, especially when the group fails to reach the threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling peer punishment in a threshold-based, multigenerational public goods game increases the probability of group success (i.e., reaching future generations and thus group-level payoff), but the efficiency gains are offset by the costs of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of peer punishment can increase the likelihood of group success, but unless punishment is low-cost or highly effective, the net effect on efficiency may be limited or null. The design dimensions—such as group size, threshold, punishment cost and effectiveness, and the absence of communication—are all well-specified and should be used to inform predictions. The results are most relevant for settings with threshold public goods, no communication, and contemporaneous peer punishment.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Acemoglu, D., & Wolitzky, A. (2020). SUSTAINING COOPERATION: COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT VERSUS SPECIALIZED ENFORCEMENT. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal way to sustain cooperation in repeated public-goods-like games depends on the effectiveness of the punishment technology. If specialized enforcers can punish at low cost and high impact, then pure enforcer punishment (with immediate return to cooperation) maximizes efficiency. If specialized enforcement is less effective, then combining enforcer punishment with community enforcement (e.g., temporary withdrawal of cooperation or ostracism) is optimal. The paper provides explicit characterizations of the maximum sustainable cooperation (efficiency) in each regime, and shows that the presence of effective specialized enforcement can increase group welfare compared to pure community enforcement. However, if the punishment technology is very weak, community enforcement alone is nearly optimal. The results generalize to both small and large groups, and to settings with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of introducing punishment in public-goods-like games, conditional on the effectiveness of the punishment technology and other game design parameters. It shows that, when punishment is highly effective (i.e., the cost to punishers is low relative to the harm inflicted), enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency by supporting higher cooperation. However, if punishment is costly or ineffective, the efficiency gain is limited, and community enforcement may be nearly as good. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency as a function of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and discount factor. The results also highlight that the structure of punishment (one-time vs. repeated, enforcer vs. peer) and the possibility of ostracism or repentance are important moderators.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Shrestha, M. D. (2021). Evolving institutions for collective action by selective imitation and self-interested design. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of collective action with institutionalized punishment by leaders, both selective imitation and self-interested design (foresight) in leaders can lead to the emergence of effective institutions that increase cooperation, group production, and payoffs (efficiency). The presence of leaders who monitor and punish free-riders increases group effort and payoffs compared to groups without leaders or punishment. The effect is strongest when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high, punishment is not too costly, and leaders value future payoffs. The model also shows that the details of the updating mechanism (imitation vs. foresight) matter most when the benefit-to-cost ratio is intermediate and leaders heavily discount the future. In 'us vs. nature' games, increased monitoring and production lead to higher payoffs for all; in 'us vs. them' games, overproduction can reduce payoffs for subordinates. The model provides direct evidence that institutionalized punishment can increase efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling institutionalized punishment by leaders in public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and punishment is not too costly. The effect is robust to variations in group size, punishment cost, and the leader's share of rewards. For prediction tasks, the presence of leader-administered punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), with the magnitude depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio, punishment cost, and whether leaders value future payoffs. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is less positive or even negative if punishment is very costly or if leaders do not value future payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with institutionalized punishment, especially when the control efficiency is low due to free-riding.

- source: Wang, M., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). The role of alliance cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with probabilistic punishment of defectors, increasing the monitoring probability and punishment severity generally increases the proportion of cooperators and can help maintain higher resource levels (i.e., higher efficiency/welfare). However, if the initial resource level is too low or the resource's maximum growth rate is insufficient, the system will experience resource depletion (tragedy of the commons) regardless of punishment strength. The model demonstrates bistability: depending on initial conditions, the system may converge to full cooperation with resource depletion, or to a mixed/cooperative state with sustainable resources. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but only when ecological conditions allow for resource regeneration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource environments is conditional: punishment (higher probability and severity) can increase efficiency and resource sustainability, but only if the resource's regenerative capacity and initial abundance are sufficient. In settings where the resource cannot recover (low growth rate or low initial stock), punishment does not prevent collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by ecological parameters (resource growth, initial abundance) in addition to standard game design dimensions. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the efficiency outcome is directly modeled.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: Góis, A. R., Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2019). Reward and punishment in climate change dilemmas. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (threshold public goods games with risk of group failure), both reward and punishment policies improve group achievement (η_G) compared to no policy. Reward is particularly effective at initiating cooperation when the risk of failure is low, while punishment is more effective at sustaining high levels of cooperation, especially when the risk is high or the threshold for group success is stringent. The optimal policy is to use reward when the fraction of cooperators is low and switch to punishment when cooperation is sufficiently high. The model provides explicit formulas for the optimal switching point and shows that combining both policies synergistically yields the highest group achievement. The results are robust across a range of parameter values, including group size, population size, and policy efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in threshold public goods games will increase group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to a control without such mechanisms. The model allows for direct mapping of game design dimensions such as player count, group size, number of rounds (in evolutionary time), presence and efficiency of punishment and reward, and the structure of the public goods dilemma (threshold, risk). The findings suggest that punishment is most effective at maintaining cooperation once it is established, while reward is more important for initiating cooperation. The optimal policy is a dynamic combination of both. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that adding punishment (especially with sufficient budget and efficiency) will generally increase efficiency, but the effect size and optimality depend on the risk, threshold, and whether reward is also available. The model is theoretical, so empirical calibration may be needed for quantitative predictions.

- source: Sun, W. W., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2021). Combination of institutional incentives for cooperative governance of risky commons. *Iscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk social dilemma games, institutional pure reward strategies (where executors only reward cooperators) are more effective than pure punishment or hybrid strategies in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target). This result holds for both fixed and flexible incentive schemes, and is robust to changes in risk level, mutation rate, and enhancement factor. Local sanctioning schemes are more effective than global ones. The main outcome is group achievement, not efficiency or total group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that, in collective-risk social dilemmas, institutional punishment alone is less effective than reward or hybrid schemes in promoting group achievement (a proxy for cooperation). However, because the main outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather the fraction of groups reaching a behavioral target, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction. The game structure (collective-risk, institutional incentives, evolutionary dynamics) is close but not identical to standard PGGs. The results suggest that institutional punishment may not increase efficiency as much as reward, but do not provide quantitative efficiency estimates.

- source: Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2019). Social evolution leads to persistent corruption. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This theoretical study shows that while institutional punishment can, in principle, sustain cooperation and high payoffs in public-goods-like settings, the possibility of corruption (i.e., punishment institutions accepting bribes) leads to cycles or stable states of reduced cooperation and lower average payoffs. The model demonstrates that transparency and monitoring are necessary to maintain honest punishment, but these are costly and thus not always sustained. As a result, the efficiency gains from punishment are not robust: corruption can cause efficiency to fall well below the cooperative optimum, and the system may oscillate between periods of high and low efficiency depending on the prevalence of corruption and monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: enabling punishment in a public-goods-like game does not guarantee increased efficiency if the punishment institution can be corrupted. The efficiency benefit of punishment depends critically on the integrity of the institution and the cost and prevalence of monitoring. In prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether corruption is possible and how transparency is maintained. If the game design allows for institutional corruption or lacks mechanisms for transparency and monitoring, enabling punishment may not improve—and may even reduce—efficiency relative to the control. The model also highlights the importance of considering dynamic and cyclical effects, not just static outcomes.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that institutions, including punishment mechanisms, can alter the payoffs in collective action problems such as public goods games, making cooperation more stable and frequent. The agent-based model demonstrates that when agents can collectively set punishment levels, cooperation can emerge and be maintained, with higher payoffs for cooperators and a reduction in free-riders. The model is illustrative and not based on empirical data. Macro-scale analyses show that institutional complexity and inclusivity are associated with higher economic development, but these are not tested in controlled PGG-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation and, in model terms, improve payoffs for cooperators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in controlled experimental settings. The agent-based model suggests that the ability to set and adjust punishment can help overcome collective action problems, but the findings are illustrative and not directly transferable to quantitative prediction tasks. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: He, N. R., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Central governance based on monitoring and reporting solves the collective-risk social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of central governance based on monitoring and reporting, with top-down punishment of defectors, can dramatically increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial collective-risk social dilemma. There are threshold effects: small increases in monitoring probability, punishment fine, or decreases in reporting cost can cause a sudden jump from low to high cooperation. The effect is stronger under harsh conditions (low synergy factor). However, the study focuses on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that top-down punishment based on monitoring and reporting can increase cooperation rates in spatial collective-risk dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial PGG-like environments, enabling punishment via monitoring/reporting is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if contributions are productive, but the paper does not quantify this. The model's details (e.g., spatial structure, overlapping groups, no explicit cost of punishment to individuals) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Korman, A., & Vacus, R. (2021). On the role of hypocrisy in escaping the tragedy of the commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in large networked public goods games with negligible MPCR, cooperation can emerge from a population of mostly defectors if mild but appropriately calibrated punishment (social-pressure) is applied, especially when an intermediate behavioral type (hypocrisy) is present. The emergence of cooperation is highly sensitive to the level of punishment against hypocritical players: too little or too much punishment leads to persistent defection or hypocrisy, while an intermediate level enables a transition to widespread cooperation. The model is theoretical and supported by numerical simulations, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the conditions under which punishment can promote cooperation in large public goods games with negligible MPCR. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the calibration of punishment levels, especially toward intermediate types like hypocrites. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment calibration and the role of intermediate behavioral types in the dynamics of cooperation.

- source: Ringsmuth, A. K., Lade, S. J., & Schlüter, M. (2019). Cross-scale cooperation enables sustainable use of a common-pool resource. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that even weak social sanctioning (punishment) between communities can prevent the collapse of cooperation that would otherwise occur due to biophysical coupling (resource sharing). When only biophysical coupling is present and communities do not communicate or sanction each other, increased resource access can undermine cooperation in one community. However, if communities can sanction defectors in both communities, or if they communicate when setting norms, cooperation is more robust. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation or defection collapses, depending on the strength of biophysical and social coupling and on whether communities collaborate in norm setting. The findings highlight the importance of cross-community sanctioning and communication for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (social sanctioning) in public-goods-like environments can prevent the collapse of cooperation that may occur due to resource sharing or biophysical coupling. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect. The results suggest that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms, as well as opportunities for communication and norm setting, are important moderators of cooperation stability. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will have a more positive effect on cooperation (and likely efficiency) when social coupling is present, especially if communities can communicate and set norms collaboratively. However, quantitative predictions about efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., & Singer, T. (2021). Cooperation across multiple game theoretical paradigms is increased by fear more than anger in selfish individuals. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: Induced fear, compared to anger, increases cooperation (as measured by a latent factor across multiple games, including the public good game) in individuals with selfish value orientations, bringing their cooperation levels up to those of prosocial individuals. No significant effect of emotion induction is found on punishment behavior. The effect is robust to controls for mood, risk attitudes, and other confounds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that emotional context (fear vs. anger) can moderate cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially among selfish individuals. However, because the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment (i.e., enabling/disabling punishment), but rather includes punishment games as part of a behavioral factor. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that context and individual differences can moderate cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Tsvetkova, M., Wagner, C., & Mao, A. (2018). The emergence of inequality in social groups: Network structure and institutions affect the distribution of earnings in cooperation games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in networked cooperation games (including PGG-like games) increases inequality in the distribution of final wealth, as measured by the Gini coefficient. This is because the cost of punishment is disproportionately borne by poorer players, who become even poorer as a result. However, the paper does not find a systematic relationship between the presence of punishment and group average wealth (i.e., efficiency or total payoff). Thus, while punishment increases cooperation and inequality, it does not reliably increase or decrease group efficiency. The main focus is on the distributional consequences (inequality), not on efficiency or total group payoff.
