  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGG-like networked cooperation games increases inequality in final wealth, but does not provide evidence that punishment systematically increases or decreases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation). For the downstream prediction task, this means that the presence of punishment should not be assumed to improve or worsen efficiency based on this evidence alone. The main effect is on the distribution of payoffs, not their sum. If the prediction task is about efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment's effect is likely neutral or context-dependent, and that other design dimensions (e.g., network structure, fluidity) may matter more for efficiency than for inequality.

- source: Redhead, D., Dhaliwal, N., & Cheng, J. T. (2021). Taking charge and stepping in: Individuals who punish are rewarded with prestige and dominance. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that high-status individuals (prestige or dominance) are more likely to engage in costly punishment in collective action settings, such as public goods games, because their status lowers the net cost and risk of punishment. Punishment can further increase their prestige or dominance through reputational rewards. The review highlights that punishment is not always altruistic and may be motivated by self-interest, especially when reputational gains are possible. The paper also notes that the net payoff of punishment depends on the balance of reputational gains and costs (including risk of retaliation), and that these dynamics are context-dependent. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual background on the social and reputational mechanisms that may moderate the effects of punishment in public goods and collective action games. It suggests that the effectiveness and cost-benefit of punishment may depend on the status of the punisher, the risk of retaliation, and the cultural or institutional context. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. Thus, while it is useful for understanding potential moderators and mechanisms, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2018). Managing and defending the commons: Experimental evidence from TURFs in Chile. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with insiders (who can communicate) and outsiders (who cannot), the introduction of poaching without monitoring or sanctions dramatically reduces insiders' earnings and resource sustainability, nearly eliminating the efficiency gains from cooperation. Adding weak external enforcement (low-probability monitoring and fines) or allowing insiders to invest in monitoring (with the same fine) both significantly reduce poaching and increase insiders' earnings and harvests, but do not fully restore efficiency to baseline (no-poaching) levels. Combining external and local monitoring does not further reduce poaching or increase efficiency. The main constraint is insiders' inability to coordinate sufficient monitoring investment. The results are robust across field (TURF members) and student samples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with communication among insiders, the introduction of punishment (via monitoring and fines for poaching) increases group efficiency relative to a poaching-only control, but the effect is partial and depends on the ability to coordinate monitoring. The effect is similar whether monitoring is provided externally or by insiders, and combining both does not yield additional efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (even if weak) should be expected to increase efficiency above the poaching-only baseline, but not to the level of the no-poaching or full-cooperation baseline. The magnitude of the effect is limited by the monitoring probability and the coordination of monitoring investment.

- source: Kosfeld, M. (2020). The role of leaders in inducing and maintaining cooperation: The CC strategy. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Drawing on a range of lab and field experiments, the paper finds that: (1) Groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators achieve better efficiency and group outcomes; (2) Punishment of non-cooperation (especially when administered by leaders with prosocial motives) increases group efficiency and cooperation, as shown in field data from Ethiopian forest user groups and lab public goods games; (3) Antisocial punishment by leaders is associated with significantly worse group outcomes; (4) Trust by leaders (as opposed to control) can increase efficiency in principal-agent settings; (5) Self-selection mechanisms can help attract motivated types, further improving group efficiency. The paper provides strong evidence that punishment, when properly targeted and motivated, is a key mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games will increase group efficiency, especially when punishment is prosocially motivated and not antisocial. The evidence is drawn from both lab and field settings, including real-world resource management and controlled experiments. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the motives of the punisher (leader or group member), the presence of motivated types, and the structure of the group. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, particularly when the punishment technology is effective and antisocial punishment is minimized. The paper also highlights the importance of leader behavior and group composition, which may interact with the game design dimensions.

- source: Bhui, R., Chudek, M., & Henrich, J. (2019). How exploitation launched human cooperation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative indirect reciprocity (NIR)—whereby individuals are allowed to exploit those with bad reputations—can suppress exploitation and support the emergence and maintenance of cooperation and public goods provision. The model shows that cooperation is most stable when exploitation is inefficient (harm to victim > gain to exploiter), and that as the frequency of opportunities for reputation improvement increases, the minimum benefit required for stable cooperation also increases. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable, and shows that NIR can serve as a foundation for more complex forms of cooperation. The overall effect of NIR is to increase group efficiency and welfare by reducing exploitation and supporting norm adherence, especially in early or cognitively simple societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, tolerated exploitation of ill-reputed individuals) can increase group efficiency and public goods provision, especially when exploitation is inefficient and when opportunities for norm adherence are not too frequent. The model gives explicit payoff-based conditions for when cooperation is stable, which can inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when the cost to the victim of exploitation is high relative to the benefit to the exploiter, and when errors in reputation assignment are low. The model also highlights that as norm adherence becomes more mandatory and frequent, the requirements for stable cooperation become stricter, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in some parameter regimes.

- source: Littman, R., Estrada, S., Stagnaro, M. N., Dunham, Y., Rand, D., & Baskin-Sommers, A. (2020). Community Violence and Prosociality: Experiencing and Committing Violence Predicts Norm-Enforcing Punishment but Not Cooperation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a high-violence urban US sample, exposure to and commission of violence are not associated with increased cooperation in economic games (including a Public Goods Game), but are positively associated with norm-enforcing punishment (i.e., willingness to pay to punish selfish others in economic games). The association is specific to norm-enforcing punishment and not to antisocial punishment or general willingness to punish. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that, in a US urban context, individuals with higher exposure to or commission of violence are more likely to engage in norm-enforcing punishment, but not more likely to cooperate. This suggests that the presence of punishment may not increase cooperation or efficiency in similar populations, but the study does not test the causal effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The design and results are most relevant for understanding individual differences in punishment and cooperation, not for predicting treatment effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Bonroy, O., Garapin, A., Hamilton, S. F., & Monteiro, D. M. S. (2019). FREE-RIDING ON PRODUCT QUALITY IN COOPERATIVES: LESSONS FROM AN EXPERIMENT. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the probability of exclusion (punishment) significantly reduces free-riding (shirking) in larger groups, and that increasing the base payoff (independent of collective returns) also reduces free-riding. In small groups, free-riding is low regardless of punishment. The main outcome is the frequency of free-riding, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment (exclusion) and higher base payoffs can facilitate cooperation, but the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (in the form of exclusion) reduces free-riding in public-goods-like environments, especially in larger groups. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (frequency of free-riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be mapped to efficiency outcomes indirectly. The experimental design and manipulation of group size, punishment probability, and base payoff are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data is a limitation. The results support the prediction that enabling punishment will increase efficiency in larger groups, especially when the probability of punishment is substantial.

- source: Bastian, B., Brewer, M., Duffy, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). From cash to crickets: The non-monetary value of a resource can promote human cooperation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that highlighting the non-monetary value of a resource (e.g., the moral significance of not harming crickets) leads to reduced personal consumption in a resource dilemma game, indicating increased cooperation. This effect is robust across two studies and is not moderated by individual differences in empathy or moral expansiveness. When a punishment mechanism is introduced (participants can pay to fine a greedy player), there is no significant difference in punishment behavior between the moral-value and control conditions. The main outcome is behavioral (points taken), not direct group efficiency or payoff, but the results suggest that moral framing can promote cooperation even in the presence of defection and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that moral or non-monetary framing of the resource can increase cooperation (reduce consumption) in resource dilemma games, which may translate to higher group efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, and the punishment mechanism (costly punishment of defectors) did not significantly affect behavior. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game design features that increase the perceived moral value of the resource may enhance cooperation, but the effect of punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper. The design is close to a public goods/common pool resource game, but not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based.

- source: Zhou, K., & Ren, T. Y. (2021). Low-carbon technology collaborative innovation in industrial cluster with social exclusion: An evolutionary game theory perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a cost to expel defectors from the public goods game) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a networked public goods environment. The effect is stronger when the cost of exclusion is low and the probability of successful exclusion is high. Sparse network structures (low average degree) are more conducive to sustaining cooperation. Voluntary participation (allowing loners) also helps prevent defection from dominating. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, social exclusion) can increase cooperation rates in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and the network is sparse. However, because the model and results focus on behavioral outcomes (cooperation level) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The model's structure and parameter explorations may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more likely to be effective, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for the prediction task.

- source: Song, Q., Cao, Z. H., Tao, R., Jiang, W., Liu, C., & Liu, J. Z. (2020). Conditional neutral punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that language and symbolic communication fundamentally alter the possibilities for collective action by simplifying coordination, reducing the costs of monitoring and punishing free riders, and amplifying reputation and signaling effects. It reviews theoretical models and empirical studies (from both ethnography and experiments) showing that communication (including chat, gossip, and feedback) increases cooperation rates and the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemmas. However, it does not report direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling communication (chat, language, gossip) in public goods games and collective action settings increases cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment, which should, in principle, improve group outcomes and efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of communication and norm establishment as moderators of punishment effectiveness.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., & Rincke, J. (2019). PUBLIC GOODS PROVISION WITH RENT-EXTRACTING ADMINISTRATORS. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study introduces a 'public trust game' (PTG), a repeated public goods game with an administrator who can extract rents from the contribution pool. The main finding is that the presence of a rent-extracting administrator reduces contributions and efficiency, but this is entirely explained by the reduction in MPCR (i.e., the efficiency of the public good). When comparing the PTG to a standard PGG with the same (lowered) MPCR, there is no significant difference in contributions or efficiency. The dynamics show that rent-extraction shocks and cooperation shocks only have short-run effects; the system returns to its previous level of cooperation and efficiency. The volatility of contributions is mainly driven by contributors' own behavior, not by the administrator. Groups with higher trust and cooperative attitudes experience less short-run volatility in cooperation after shocks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where an administrator can extract rents, the negative effect on efficiency is fully mediated by the reduction in MPCR. If the control game (standard PGG) and the treatment game (with administrator) have the same MPCR, the presence of the administrator does not further reduce efficiency. Thus, for prediction, the key moderator is the effective MPCR: punishment or rent extraction that reduces MPCR will reduce efficiency accordingly, but the mere presence of an administrator or rent-extraction opportunity does not further depress efficiency beyond this. The paper does not study punishment in the standard sense (peer or centralized costly punishment), but its findings are highly informative for cases where punishment or corruption reduces the effective return from cooperation.

- source: Gross, J., & Böhm, R. (2020). Voluntary restrictions on self-reliance increase cooperation and mitigate wealth inequality. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can solve a collective action problem either individually (self-reliance) or collectively (public good), the availability of self-reliance crowds out cooperation and increases inequality, especially when self-reliance is relatively cheap. However, when groups are given the opportunity to vote to abolish the individual solution, they overwhelmingly do so under medium and high interdependence, which leads to higher rates of public goods provision, lower resource waste, higher group earnings, and reduced inequality. The effect is particularly strong when there is a risk of miscoordination (medium interdependence) or when group members differ in their ability to be self-reliant (wealth heterogeneity). The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the standard sense, but self-reliance by richer group members can function as a coercive device, pressuring poorer members to cooperate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional design features that restrict individual opt-out/self-reliance options can substantially increase efficiency and group earnings in public-goods-like environments, especially when the efficiency gains from cooperation are significant (i.e., when self-reliance is costly relative to the public good). While the study does not manipulate punishment, it shows that endogenous institutional restrictions (analogous to mandatory participation or exclusion of exit options) can have large positive effects on efficiency, similar to or greater than those observed for punishment in standard PGGs. For prediction, the presence or absence of self-reliance/exit options, and the ability to restrict them, should be considered as key moderators of efficiency, alongside standard game design dimensions.

- source: Nyborg, K. (2018). Reciprocal climate negotiators. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocal preferences can, under certain conditions (strong and widespread reciprocity), stabilize the grand coalition in a public-goods-like coalition game, leading to high group welfare (efficiency). If only a minority of countries are reciprocal, or if reciprocity is weak, the only stable coalitions are small and provide little welfare improvement over the non-cooperative baseline. The model demonstrates that the presence of reciprocal preferences can shift the game from a pure Prisoner's Dilemma to a coordination game in utilities, but this does not guarantee high efficiency unless reciprocity is strong and common. The paper does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning institutions, but reciprocity can act as a form of endogenous social sanctioning. The welfare/efficiency effects of reciprocity are thus mixed: potentially large if conditions are right, but often minimal in realistic parameter ranges.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that social preferences (reciprocity) can, in principle, increase efficiency in public-goods-like coalition games, but only if they are strong and widespread. In the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms, reciprocity alone is not a reliable path to high efficiency unless the population is highly reciprocal. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling social preference mechanisms (like reciprocity) may only improve efficiency if the relevant parameters (strength and prevalence of reciprocity) are high. The model does not address explicit punishment, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled games is limited, but it does show that endogenous social preferences can sometimes substitute for formal sanctions in supporting cooperation and efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented via social preferences rather than institutionalized mechanisms.

- source: Chen, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Grimshaw, S. B. (2021). When a nudge is (not) enough: Experiments on social information and incentives. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In the large-N binary prisoners' dilemma, both financial incentives (reward or fine) and positive social information (majority invest) increase cooperation rates among unmotivated types. Negative social information (majority keep) reduces cooperation among motivated types. The combination of incentives and positive social information does not produce additional gains for unmotivated types, and may even crowd out intrinsic motivation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results imply that punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, especially among those not intrinsically motivated to cooperate. The effect of social information is context-dependent: it is more effective when the externality of cooperation is salient and financially quantifiable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of punishment/reward mechanisms and social information on cooperation rates in a large-N binary prisoners' dilemma with MPCR = 0.05. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation rates from punishment/reward treatments among unmotivated types suggests that enabling punishment/reward would likely increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the externality is salient. The design dimensions (large group, binary choice, no chat, no explicit payoff feedback beyond own outcome, known group size, no identification of punishers) are all specified. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment/reward increases cooperation and likely efficiency, especially among defectors.

- source: De Luca, G., Sekeris, P. G., & Spengler, D. E. (2018). Can violence harm cooperation? Experimental evidence. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments: a 'conflict' treatment where violent appropriation (chance) is a viable option when resources are low, and a 'control' treatment where the cost of appropriation is so high that it is never optimal. In the conflict treatment, participants are significantly less likely to choose the cooperative extraction rate ('low') and more likely to choose the non-cooperative rate ('high') in early rounds, anticipating future conflict. The expectation of future conflict thus undermines cooperation even before conflict occurs. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral shift from cooperation to non-cooperation implies lower efficiency in the conflict treatment. Additional analyses show that experiencing conflict in previous games further reduces cooperation in subsequent games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a credible, destructive appropriation/conflict option in a dynamic CPR game reduces cooperative behavior and likely reduces efficiency, even before conflict is actually chosen. For prediction tasks, the presence of a credible destructive sanction (even if not a standard punishment mechanism) should be expected to reduce efficiency relative to a control with no such option. However, the paper does not study standard punishment (costly peer punishment for norm violations) but rather a destructive appropriation/conflict option, so the results are most relevant for settings where punishment is destructive and ends the game, not for standard public goods games with ongoing punishment. The findings suggest that the threat of destructive sanctions can unravel cooperation and efficiency, especially when the sanction is credible and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.

- source: Pedroso, M. (2021). Blind Cooperation: The Evolution of Redundancy via Ignorance. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group cooperation can evolve without targeted punishment or coercion if individuals are ignorant of others' strategies and/or face harsh environments. In a threshold public goods game, when players do not know what others will do, they cooperate with an intermediate probability, leading to more cooperators than strictly necessary (redundancy). Harsher environments (higher group-level cost of failure) increase the probability of cooperation and thus redundancy. In contrast, if individuals are well-informed about others' strategies, redundancy does not evolve without punishment. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, but discusses these concepts in relation to the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how information availability and environmental harshness affect cooperation and redundancy in threshold public goods games. It suggests that punishment is not strictly necessary for redundancy or intermediate cooperation rates if players are ignorant of others' actions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of information and environmental context as moderators, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Senthilnathan, A., & Gavrilets, S. (2021). The dynamics of cooperation, power, and inequality in a group-structured society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and efficiency in group-structured societies depend strongly on group size, the degree of rivalrousness of goods, and the strength of institutional checks and balances (modeled by the incumbency parameter ε). Stronger checks and balances (lower ε) promote cooperation and reduce inequality. Larger groups tend to be more powerful and successful in between-group cooperation, except when goods are highly rivalrous, in which case smaller groups may outperform larger ones (Olson's paradox). The model predicts multiple stable equilibria and non-equilibrium dynamics depending on parameter values. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; these are only discussed as possible future extensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group size, benefit parameters, and rivalrousness affect efficiency and payoff outcomes in nested public-goods-like environments. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of structural and institutional moderators, but not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Hu, L. W., He, N. R., Weng, Q. F., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2020). Rewarding endowments lead to a win-win in the evolution of public cooperation and the accumulation of common resources. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: Rewarding endowments in a collective-risk social dilemma (a PGG variant with environmental feedback) can create a win-win for cooperation and resource accumulation, but only in a specific parameter region: low basic endowment and intermediate reward intensity. High reward intensity or high basic endowment can lead to resource exhaustion, even if cooperation is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctions. The main outcomes are the amount of common resources (group welfare) and a composite index of cooperation and resource accumulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it studies only reward mechanisms and does not include punishment or sanctions. However, it provides evidence that reward mechanisms can increase both cooperation and group welfare, but only under certain parameter regimes. The findings suggest that the design of incentive mechanisms (reward intensity, basic endowment) can have non-monotonic effects on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of reward as a moderator or alternative.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Yamamoto, T., Ito, H., Nii, M., Okabe, T., Morita, S., & Yoshimura, J. (2019). A single 'weight-lifting' game covers all kinds of games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper demonstrates mathematically that the weight-lifting game can represent all major two-player cooperation/defection games by adjusting three parameters: the incremental success probabilities from cooperation and the cost-to-benefit ratio. It provides a unified payoff matrix and phase diagrams showing how efficiency and cooperation depend on these parameters. The model is shown to encompass the standard public goods game as a special case when the success probability increases linearly with the number of cooperators. The paper does not consider punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how efficiency and group payoff outcomes in public-goods-like games depend on the underlying payoff structure, especially the cost/benefit ratio and the synergy of cooperation. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanism, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for baseline modeling of efficiency in the absence of punishment, or for understanding how the structure of the game (e.g., synergy, cost/benefit) sets the stage for potential intervention effects.

- source: Hackel, J., Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., Goto, A., & Taudes, A. (2021). Asymmetric effects of social and economic incentives on cooperation in real effort based public goods games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that social incentives (charity donations) significantly increase the proportion of participants who fully cooperate (complete 90+ tasks), while economic incentives (personal monetary reward) significantly reduce the proportion of full defectors (complete 10 or fewer tasks). The effects are asymmetric: social incentives promote high cooperation, economic incentives reduce non-cooperation. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, focusing instead on positive incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The design is a close variant of a PGG, using real effort instead of resource allocation, and focuses on the effects of positive (economic and social) incentives on cooperation and defection rates. For prediction tasks about punishment and efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that incentive structures (other than punishment) can have asymmetric effects on cooperation and defection. The absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff behavioral outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under punishment.

- source: Chambers, C. R., & Baker, W. E. (2020). Robust Systems of Cooperation in the Presence of Rankings: How Displaying Prosocial Contributions Can Offset the Disruptive Effects of Performance Rankings. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing performance rankings tied to differential rewards causes a sharp and persistent decline in cooperation (measured as transfer rates) in a generalized reciprocity game. This effect is especially pronounced in groups that were highly cooperative before the intervention. When reputation information (about others' past giving) is also provided, the initial drop in cooperation is similar, but cooperation rates recover over time to pre-intervention levels. The mechanism appears to be that actors use reputation information to reward those who have been cooperative, especially when they perceive inequity. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding how performance rankings and reputation information affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but the outcome of interest is cooperation/transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding the impact of competitive incentives and reputation mechanisms on cooperation.

- source: Xu, Z. J., Li, R. Y., & Zhang, L. Z. (2019). The role of memory in human strategy updating in optional public goods game. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a spatial optional public goods game, human players update their strategies primarily based on their own past experiences (memory), not by imitating neighbors. The probability of switching to a strategy is higher if it performed well for the individual in the past, regardless of its performance among neighbors. Imitation and mutation dynamics do not adequately explain observed behavior. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its main contribution is to the understanding of human strategy updating (memory vs. imitation) in repeated spatial PGGs with voluntary participation. It is not informative for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Barclay, P., & Barker, J. L. (2020). Greener Than Thou: People who protect the environment are more cooperative, compete to be environmental, and benefit from reputation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) people who engage in more pro-environmental behaviors are more likely to cooperate in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma; (2) people donate more to an environmental charity when their donations are observed, and even more when competing to be chosen as a partner; (3) observers preferentially choose and cooperate more with those who donated more to the environment; (4) the correlation between environmentalism and cooperation is partially mediated by concern for others. The study does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of observability and partner choice (reputation/competition) on cooperative and pro-environmental behavior in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For prediction tasks involving the effects of observability or partner choice on cooperation, the findings suggest that such mechanisms can increase cooperative behaviors, but no efficiency or payoff data are available.

- source: Gallier, C., Langbein, J., & Vance, C. (2018). Non-binding Restrictions, Cooperation, and Coral Reef Protection: Experimental Evidence from Indonesian Fishing Communities. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that non-binding recommendations, especially those from an external source (e.g., NGO or scientist), can reduce extraction effort in a CPR game, but this effect is highly context-dependent and only significant in the most diverse community (Bunaken). Democratic recommendations have a weaker effect, and leader-based recommendations have no effect. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The main outcome is extraction effort, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that, in the absence of enforcement, non-binding recommendations alone are often insufficient to promote cooperation, except in certain socio-cultural contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the limits of non-binding recommendations in the absence of enforcement, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment means this study is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Hajikhameneh, A., & Rubin, J. (2019). Exchange in the Absence of Legal Enforcement: Reputation and Multilateral Punishment under Uncertainty. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with 12 principals and 12 agents per session, a multilateral punishment institution allows principals to assign a permanent bad reputation to agents at a cost (C=0 or C=5). The experiment varies the probability that the principal's return is destroyed by nature (θ=0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.30). Total welfare (sum of principal and agent earnings) is the main outcome. When punishment is costless, welfare decreases monotonically with uncertainty. When punishment is costly, welfare is not significantly different between no uncertainty and modest uncertainty (θ=0.05), but falls at higher uncertainty. The cost of punishment reduces the frequency of punishment and can lead to more 'benefit of the doubt' given to agents with bad reputations, which can offset welfare losses from uncertainty at low levels. However, at high uncertainty, welfare declines regardless of punishment cost. The design does not allow for endogenous institution formation or communication between players.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how the cost of punishment and the presence of outcome uncertainty moderate the effect of multilateral punishment on efficiency in a trust-game-like environment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment can sustain high efficiency when uncertainty is low and punishment is not too costly, but that the effect is attenuated or reversed as uncertainty increases or punishment becomes more costly. The results are most relevant for trust games with binary reputation and costly, permanent punishment, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs. The findings support the importance of including punishment cost and environmental uncertainty as moderators when predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Li, X. L., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., Li, H. J., Shi, L., Podobnik, B., Stanley, H. E., Havlin, S., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Punishment diminishes the benefits of network reciprocity in social dilemma experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared three treatments: (1) a well-mixed repeated PD (control), (2) a static network PD with two neighbors (network reciprocity), and (3) a static network PD with punishment available. Network reciprocity increased both cooperation and payoff relative to the well-mixed control. However, adding punishment did not further increase cooperation and actually reduced the median payoff per round back to the control level. Punishment also reduced assortment (clustering of cooperators) and, in larger neighborhoods, erased the positive correlation between cooperation and payoff. Thus, punishment in this setting is ineffective or even counterproductive for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games on static networks (with two or four neighbors), enabling costly punishment (with a 1:4 cost-to-impact ratio) does not improve and may even reduce group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The negative effect is especially clear in payoff-based outcomes: median payoff per round is lower with punishment than without, and the benefit of network reciprocity is erased. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar networked PD or adjacent PGG-like environments, adding punishment may not increase efficiency and could reduce it, especially as neighborhood size increases. The findings are most directly applicable to static network PDs with small groups, no communication, and fixed payoff matrices.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation. The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2020). Collective punishment promotes resource conservation if it is not enforced. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when participants can voluntarily contribute to a centralized sanctioning institution (which probabilistically punishes untrustworthy behavior in a dyadic trust game), they frequently do so, but the resulting institutions are typically inefficient. The cost of forming the institution is not offset by the gains in trust and trustworthiness, so overall group earnings (efficiency) are lower than in the control condition where no sanctioning institution can be formed. This result holds for both partner and stranger matching protocols. The paper also finds that higher sanction probabilities increase trust and trustworthiness, but the aggregate welfare is still lower due to the cost of institution formation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game environment with voluntary centralized sanctioning, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline, even though trust and trustworthiness increase. For prediction, this suggests that the presence of costly, voluntarily funded centralized punishment may decrease efficiency unless the punishment mechanism is highly cost-effective. The findings are most relevant for trust-game-like settings with endogenous institution formation, and less directly for standard PGGs, but the negative efficiency effect of costly punishment is a cautionary signal for similar environments.

- source: Bhaskar, V., & Thomas, C. (2019). Community Enforcement of Trust with Bounded Memory. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation (trust/lending) in large societies with random matching and one-sided moral hazard depends critically on the information structure available to punishers (lenders). With perfect bounded memory, punishment unravels and efficiency collapses. With coarse information (binary flag for recent default), temporary exclusion can sustain high efficiency, with the exact level depending on the profitability of lending and the exclusion length. The model provides explicit formulas for borrower and lender payoffs under different regimes. When lending is very profitable, some inefficiency is unavoidable due to the need for randomization and endogenous adverse selection. The results generalize to other sequential-move games with one-sided moral hazard.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the information structure: coarse information (pooling recent defaulters) enables efficient punishment and high payoffs, while fine-grained information leads to unraveling and zero efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will only improve efficiency if the punishment mechanism is supported by an information structure that prevents cherry-picking of near-rehabilitated defectors. The model is not a PGG or direct PGG variant, but the logic applies to trust games and other one-sided moral hazard settings. The findings are most relevant for settings with random matching, temporary exclusion, and information constraints.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Berger, U., & De Silva, H. (2021). Evolution of deterrence with costly reputation information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that in a repeated two-player deterrence game (with roles of challenger and defender), the possibility of costly punishment (retaliation) can lead to higher defender payoffs and social welfare, but only when reputation information is minimal (e.g., only the last action is observable and costly to obtain). When reputation information is rich (e.g., challengers can observe a defender's empirical fighting frequency over many rounds), deterrence breaks down, punishment loses its deterrent effect, and defender payoffs collapse. Agent-based simulations confirm that defender payoffs decline as the amount of reputation information increases, with a sharp drop when information becomes too rich. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: positive under minimal information, negative or null under rich information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the information structure: punishment increases efficiency (defender payoffs) when reputation information is minimal and costly, but not when information is rich and easily available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension 'information about others' past actions' (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' or 'show_punishment_id') is a key moderator: enabling rich, low-cost information can undermine the efficiency benefits of punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player deterrence games, but the mechanisms may generalize to PGG variants where punishment and reputation interact.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Barron, D., & Guo, Y. N. (2021). THE USE AND MISUSE OF COORDINATED PUNISHMENTS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in relational/trust games with coordinated punishment, the possibility of extortion (i.e., agents threatening to trigger punishment unless paid, regardless of their effort) can completely undermine cooperation and efficiency. In the baseline model with extortion, equilibrium effort and surplus are zero. However, if institutions can tie the ability to trigger punishment (leverage) to actual effort—such as through public signals of effort or strong dyadic relationships—then positive effort and efficiency can be restored, though often at the cost of rents to agents or the need for strong relationships.
