The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore not universally positive: it depends critically on whether misuse/extortion is possible and whether institutional features can prevent it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games is highly sensitive to the possibility of extortion and the institutional context. If punishment can be misused (e.g., via action-contingent threats), enabling punishment may reduce efficiency to zero. However, if the game design includes mechanisms that tie punishment power to actual cooperative behavior (e.g., public effort signals, strong dyadic relationships), punishment can support high efficiency. For prediction, this means that the presence or absence of extortion opportunities, and the presence of institutional safeguards, are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Game design dimensions that affect the ability to make credible threats, the observability of effort, and the structure of relationships are especially important.

- source: Zhao, Y. K., Zhao, X. B., & Shen, Z. J. M. (2018). The hot-versus-cold effect in a punishment game: a multi-round experimental study. *Annals of Operations Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two elicitation methods (strategy vs. direct-response) in a multi-round, two-player punishment game. The strategy method leads to significantly higher punishment rates (84% vs. 62%) and more frequent cooperative sender actions (S1: 68% vs. 44%). Combined payoffs (group welfare) are higher for S1 actions, and the higher frequency of S1 under the strategy method results in higher average group payoffs. Regression and learning model analyses show that the difference in punishment rates is due to mental accounting: the strategy method allows receivers to integrate the cost and expected benefit of punishment, increasing its perceived utility. Over repeated rounds, senders learn to avoid punishment by choosing S1 more often, further increasing group welfare. Reward rates are low and do not differ between methods. The findings suggest that, in repeated games, the strategic value of punishment (deterrence) is more important than the immediate emotional satisfaction of punishing, and that the design of the punishment mechanism (including decision timing) can significantly affect cooperation and group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player punishment games, higher punishment rates (enabled by the strategy method) lead to more cooperative sender behavior and higher group payoffs. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the structure is adjacent: one player can take a selfish or cooperative action, and the other can punish. The results suggest that, in repeated settings, enabling effective punishment (with high perceived strategic value) increases efficiency by deterring selfish behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that, all else equal, punishment mechanisms that increase the perceived strategic value of punishment (e.g., via decision timing or repeated interaction) will lead to higher efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low due to frequent selfish actions. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, punishment-enabled games, but the mechanism may generalize to other repeated social dilemmas with punishment.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Explaining the evolution of parochial punishment in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment can promote cooperation and increase group average payoff (efficiency) when it is targeted at cheats and used to enforce cooperation. However, the evolution of parochial punishment (harsher punishment of out-group cheats) depends on the ecological scenario. Parochial punishment evolves (and can support high between-group cooperation and group payoff) when groups interact collaboratively and conflict occurs with random groups, or when cooperation is difficult to enforce. In contrast, when conflict occurs with the same group as prior interactions, punishment becomes spiteful (targeting all out-groups, including cooperators), which undermines between-group cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model shows that both collaborative intergroup relations and the structure of conflict are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive (by promoting cooperation) or negative (by reducing group payoff through excessive or misdirected punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, especially in settings with group structure, third-party punishment, and group-level selection. It shows that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether punishment is targeted at cheats (promoting cooperation and group payoff) or is spiteful (reducing group payoff by punishing all out-groups). The presence and structure of intergroup conflict, the difficulty of enforcing cooperation, and the ability to form collaborative intergroup relationships are key moderators. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when it is used to enforce cooperation (especially when cooperation is hard to sustain), but can reduce efficiency if it is misdirected or used spitefully. The findings are most relevant for games with group structure, repeated interactions, and third-party punishment, but less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without these features.

- source: Zhang, M., Ma, Y. J., Tao, Y., Wang, Z., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2020). Awareness of wealth inequalities breeds animosity. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in repeated PD games with costly punishment, the visibility of wealth information interacts with initial wealth inequality to affect both cooperation rates and average final wealth. When initial wealth is unequal, making wealth visible reduces cooperation and leads to lower average final wealth compared to when wealth is hidden. When initial wealth is equal, visibility has little effect on cooperation or final wealth. Punishment is available but rarely used, and its frequency does not significantly differ across treatments. Thus, in environments with inequality, hiding wealth information improves both cooperation and group payoff (final wealth), while visibility exacerbates inequality and reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving efficiency in repeated PD or PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides evidence that the visibility of wealth information is a strong moderator when initial endowments are unequal: making wealth visible reduces efficiency (average final wealth), while hiding it increases efficiency. The effect is not present when initial endowments are equal. The presence of punishment does not appear to moderate this effect, as punishment is infrequent and not significantly different across treatments. Thus, when modeling efficiency outcomes, consider both initial wealth inequality and information visibility as key moderators, especially in games with available but rarely used punishment.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Matsuzawa, R., & Tanimoto, J. (2018). Sanctions triggered by jealousy help promote the cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that jealousy-motivated sanctions (where agents punish neighbors with higher payoffs, regardless of strategy) can promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect depends on the network structure: in lattice networks, higher sanction efficiency (penalty per cost) promotes cooperation, while in scale-free networks, lower efficiency (higher cost per penalty) can be more effective. However, the paper notes that even when cooperation is achieved, the continued use of jealous sanctions (due to persistent payoff inequalities) can reduce total social payoff, potentially lowering efficiency. The main reported outcome is the cooperation fraction, not direct group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to the cost of sanctions, especially when punishment is motivated by jealousy and can be directed at cooperators as well as defectors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to depend on network structure and the cost-effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment may increase cooperation but could reduce efficiency if sanctioning is costly and not well-targeted.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: Nese, A., O'Higgins, N., Sbriglia, P., & Scudiero, M. (2018). Cooperation, punishment and organized crime: a lab-in-the-field experiment in southern Italy. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Camorra inmates are highly cooperative and strongly punish defectors, consistent with their internal codes of conduct. However, when external punishment (third-party punishment) is introduced, their cooperation decreases, indicating a rejection of external authority. Ordinary criminals, in contrast, increase cooperation when external punishment is possible, suggesting opportunism. Students' cooperation also decreases with TPP. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings highlight the importance of group-specific social norms and attitudes in shaping responses to punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how the introduction of punishment affects cooperation rates in a Prisoner's Dilemma setting, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player PD and a three-player PD with third-party punishment, rather than a standard multi-player PGG. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly on efficiency) is highly dependent on group norms and attitudes: punishment can reduce cooperation in some groups (students, Camorra) and increase it in others (ordinary criminals). However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment under different social norms, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference. The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Chen, H. Z., Zeng, Z. J., & Ma, J. H. (2020). The source of punishment matters: Third-party punishment restrains observers from selfish behaviors better than does second-party punishment by shaping norm perceptions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) of unfair behavior in a dictator game leads observers to perceive higher descriptive and injunctive norms for sharing, and to share more themselves, compared to second-party punishment (SPP). Mediation analyses show that the effect on sharing is primarily through changes in descriptive norm perception. The effect is robust across two experiments with different manipulations (verbal blame and monetary punishment). However, the outcome is individual sharing behavior, not group efficiency or payoff, and the context is a dictator game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the source of punishment (third-party vs. second-party) can affect observers' prosocial behavior by shaping norm perceptions, especially descriptive norms. While the findings are robust for sharing behavior in dictator games, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment mechanisms that are perceived as norm-expressive (e.g., third-party punishment) may have stronger effects on prosocial behavior, but the transferability to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is indirect and should be treated with caution.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Doering, L., & Ody-Brasier, A. (2021). Time and Punishment: How Individuals Respond to Being Sanctioned in Voluntary Associations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effect of being sanctioned (fined) for absence in voluntary microsavings groups changes over time. Early in group tenure, sanctioned individuals reduce their prosocial contributions to the collective fund, but as they become more dependent on the group (measured by time in the group or by having received emergency funds), their response to sanctions becomes increasingly prosocial, with higher allocations to the collective fund after being fined. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative specifications. The study does not measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but the findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting prosocial behavior is temporally and relationally dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on prosocial behavior (here, contributions to a collective fund) is not static but depends on group tenure and individual dependence on the group. For prediction tasks involving efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments, this suggests that the impact of punishment may be weaker or even negative early in group formation, but can become more positive as group members become more dependent on each other. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather individual prosocial allocation, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The study is most informative about the temporal dynamics and social context moderating punishment effects, rather than about the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Moral labels increase cooperation and costly punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that moral framing (using labels like 'I cooperate' and 'I cheat') increases the likelihood of both moral and hypocritical punishment compared to neutral framing ('A' and 'B'). In Experiment 1, where only participants could punish, moral framing increased punishment but did not affect cooperation. In Experiment 2, where both participants and partners could punish, moral framing increased both punishment and cooperation. Antisocial punishment and general punishment bias were not affected by framing. The results suggest that the effect of moral framing on cooperation is contingent on the presence of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and framing of punishment options can increase both punishment behavior and, under certain conditions, cooperation in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage, but the findings about behavioral responses to punishment and framing may be informative for predicting when punishment will increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) in similar environments. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to increase cooperation when it is symmetric (i.e., both players can punish), and that moral framing amplifies this effect.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Otto, A. R., & Phelps, E. A. (2018). Learning Moral Values: Another's Desire to Punish Enhances One's Own Punitive Behavior. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four experiments, participants who learned about another's strong preference for punishment increased their own endorsement of punitive options when responding to unfair offers. This effect was observed both when participants actively implemented another's preferences and when they passively observed them. The effect generalized across online and lab settings, and from a justice game (with compensatory and punitive options) to the ultimatum game (where only costly punishment is available). The learning process is best described by a reinforcement learning model sensitive to the magnitude of unfairness. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare; the focus is on individual behavioral change in punitive choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that exposure to others' punitive preferences increases individuals' own willingness to punish in response to unfairness, even when punishment is costly. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is dyadic and focused on justice restoration, not multi-player public goods provision. The findings are most relevant for predicting changes in punitive behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Sun, C. B., & Luo, C. (2020). Co-evolution of influence-based preferential selection and limited resource with multi-games on interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that female water point leaders (WPLs) in rural Namibia are younger and better educated than male WPLs, but are often related to traditional elites. In a trust game with third-party punishment, female WPLs punish less frequently (not statistically significant) and significantly less intensively than male WPLs. However, these differences in punishment behavior do not translate into differences in villager satisfaction with leaders. The experiment does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on gender differences in third-party punishment in a trust game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It shows that female leaders punish less, but this does not affect satisfaction. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design and findings may be useful for understanding how leader characteristics affect norm enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Behnk, S., Barreda-Tarrazona, I., & García-Gallego, A. (2018). Punishing liars-How monitoring affects honesty and trust. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a severe, cost-free punishment mechanism for dishonesty in a sender-receiver game leads to a large increase in honest messages and trust by receivers, compared to a baseline with no punishment. This effect persists even when the probability of detecting dishonesty is reduced to 10%, except at a moderate detection probability (25%), where honesty is lower. The deterrence effect of punishment is less pronounced when the sender's potential gain from deception is high. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the behavioral results indicate that punishment can sustain high levels of honesty and trust with minimal monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a principal-agent (sender-receiver) environment with asymmetric information, the availability of severe, cost-free punishment for dishonesty can dramatically increase honest behavior and trust, even with low monitoring (detection) probabilities. However, the game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, and efficiency or group payoff is not measured. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, the results suggest that punishment mechanisms with high severity and low cost may have strong positive effects on pro-social behavior, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGGs is indirect. The findings are most relevant for settings where the main concern is honesty/truth-telling under asymmetric information, rather than contribution to a public good.

- source: Vollan, B., Blanco, E., Steimanis, I., Petutschnig, F., & Prediger, S. (2020). Procedural fairness and nepotism among local traditional and democratic leaders in rural Namibia. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both traditional chiefs and democratically elected leaders in rural Namibia are similarly likely to implement democratic decision-making procedures and are similarly unlikely to engage in nepotistic punishment. Chiefs may be slightly more likely to choose democratic procedures and less likely to be lenient toward relatives in punishment, but these differences are not statistically robust. The main outcomes are behavioral (procedural choice, nepotism in punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The punishment task is a trust game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game. The study suggests that, in this context of legal pluralism, traditional leaders are not more autocratic or nepotistic than elected leaders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on punishment behavior (altruistic punishment) and procedural fairness in a trust game with third-party punishment, not in a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does show that, in a real-world setting, leaders' willingness to punish norm violations is not strongly affected by whether they are democratically elected or traditional chiefs, and that nepotism in punishment is low for both. The design and context differ from standard PGGs, so the transfer value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited.

- source: Lo Gerfo, E., Gallucci, A., Morese, R., Vergallito, A., Ottone, S., Ponzano, F., Locatelli, G., Bosco, F., & Lauro, L. J. R. (2019). The role of ventromedial prefrontal cortex and temporo-parietal junction in third-party punishment behavior. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that modulating neural activity in the VMPFC increases the tendency to punish unfair behavior (altruistic punishment), while modulating the TPJ increases the tendency to punish fair behavior (antisocial punishment) in a third-party punishment game. The behavioral outcomes are measured as the decision to punish and the amount of punishment, not as group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report on the effect of punishment on group welfare, efficiency, or aggregate earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to individual punishment behavior in a third-party context, with no analysis of how punishment affects group outcomes or efficiency. The game is a modified Dictator Game, not a standard PGG or repeated PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as context for the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior.

- source: Bartos, V., & Levely, I. (2021). Sanctioning and trustworthiness across ethnic groups: Experimental evidence from Afghanistan. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of scarcity (lean season), participants are significantly less willing to engage in costly third-party punishment to enforce sharing norms, as measured by a lower minimum acceptable offer (MAO) in the TPPG. However, the amount shared by dictators (Player A) in both the DG and TPPG remains stable across seasons, indicating that sharing behavior is robust to scarcity even as enforcement weakens. The results are robust to various controls and alternative explanations. No direct analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations (i.e., enforce sharing) is sensitive to resource scarcity, but actual sharing behavior is not. However, because the design is a one-shot dictator/TPPG (not a repeated public goods game), and because no group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment may become less frequent or intense under scarcity, but this does not necessarily reduce sharing in the short run. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be context-dependent and potentially weaker under scarcity, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gambetta, D., & Przepiorka, W. (2019). Sharing Compromising Information as a Cooperative Strategy. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when a punishment mechanism is available (i.e., when norm-enforcement is possible), cooperation among less cooperative types ('hawks') increases, especially if both parties can mutually reveal their compromising information and thus make themselves vulnerable to punishment. In conditions without punishment, hawks rarely cooperate with each other. The effect is strongest when both the revelation of compromising information and the ability to punish are present and mutually chosen. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, information sharing), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment opportunities can enable cooperation among otherwise uncooperative individuals, but the paper does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a trust-game-like environment can increase cooperation among less cooperative individuals, especially when mutual vulnerability is possible. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task. The game is a one-shot trust game variant, not a standard repeated public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured. The punishment mechanism is costly and only available in certain conditions. The results suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains (if any) is not reported.

- source: Xie, Y. Y., Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., & Bu, H. Y. (2020). Impact of binary social status with hierarchical punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing only a subset of agents (the 'powerful' group) to punish defectors can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently high. However, the relationship is non-linear: too many powerful agents or excessively strong punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of hierarchical punishment on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that the structure and exclusivity of punishment rights, as well as the intensity of punishment, can have non-monotonic effects on cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting similar mechanisms in public goods games. Use this paper for qualitative or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Promoting cooperation by punishing minority. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a multilayer network with inter-layer feedback and reinforcement learning, cooperation is promoted by lower punishment costs and higher punishment severity. The frequency of cooperation is sensitive to these parameters, with cooperation collapsing when punishment is too costly or too weak. The self-adapting Q-learning mechanism enables agents to learn strategies that support higher cooperation rates, especially when learning rates and discount factors are tuned appropriately. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost and severity affect cooperation rates in a spatially structured, multilayer environment with reinforcement learning. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation under certain cost/severity regimes, but excessive punishment cost can undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results are only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify group payoff or efficiency. The model is also not a standard public goods game, but rather a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment administered by a management layer.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2018). Heterogeneous groups overcome the diffusion of responsibility problem in social norm enforcement. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that diffusion of responsibility (lower individual sanctioning rates as group size increases) is present in symmetric groups but is largely overcome in heterogeneous groups where one member has a lower cost of sanctioning. Over repeated rounds, groups tacitly coordinate on the strong member to sanction, leading to higher and more efficient norm enforcement. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (sanctioning rates), not payoff-based efficiency or group welfare. The findings suggest that group heterogeneity can mitigate the bystander effect in norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence on how this affects group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how group size and heterogeneity in punishment cost affect the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement in a volunteer's dilemma context. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings suggest that introducing heterogeneity in punishment cost can increase the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement, especially in larger groups, but the translation to efficiency or welfare outcomes in standard PGGs is indirect.

- source: Hu, K. P., Tao, Y. W., Ma, Y. J., & Shi, L. (2021). Peer pressure induced punishment resolves social dilemma on interdependent networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a peer-pressure-like punishment mechanism—where players with inconsistent strategies across interdependent network layers have their payoffs reduced—can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much punishment is less effective. The effect is robust to changes in network structure (lattice, small-world, random) and the degree of payoff interdependence between layers. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; the main focus is on cooperation rates and the spatial distribution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, especially when the punishment is moderate. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that moderate punishment is likely to be most effective for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. The design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment cost (modeled as a reduction in payoff for inconsistent pairs) and the degree of interdependence between players' payoffs. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Steimanis, I., Hofmann, R., Mbidzo, M., & Vollan, B. (2020). When female leaders believe that men make better leaders: Empowerment in community-based water management in rural Namibia. *Journal of Rural Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of conditional neutral punishment—where agents punish neighbors with the opposite strategy if their own payoff is below the neighborhood average—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the cost and effectiveness of punishment. Cooperative punishers form protective clusters that shield cooperators, while antisocial punishment (defectors punishing cooperators) is largely ineffective. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional neutral punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically—but not always—imply higher efficiency, especially in prisoner's dilemma-like settings. However, without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the magnitude and reliability of efficiency gains from punishment in this design cannot be directly inferred. The spatial structure, conditionality of punishment, and the absence of communication or reward mechanisms are important contextual features.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., & Fu, F. (2018). Strategy intervention for the evolution of fairness. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of fairness in the Ultimatum Game is promoted by the presence of spiteful strategies (which reject low or unequal offers, i.e., costly punishment) and inhibited by altruistic strategies (which accept any offer). The introduction of non-monotonic rejection (rejecting both low and high offers) further helps fairness overcome selfishness. In group-structured populations, larger population size increases the frequency of altruism and decreases the frequency of selfishness and spitefulness, but does not change the dominance of fairness. Migration inhibits the evolution of selfishness and fairness, but promotes altruism and, with sufficient migration, spitefulness. Mutation has non-monotonic effects, with intermediate mutation maximizing fairness and selfishness, and minimizing spitefulness. The main dependent variable is the frequency of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of costly punishment (rejection) in promoting fairness in strategic environments similar to the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it supports the idea that the availability of costly punishment can increase the prevalence of fairness-oriented behavior, especially when non-monotonic punishment is possible. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment mechanisms affect the evolutionary stability of fairness, not for predicting group efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Complex evolutionary dynamics due to punishment and free space in ecological multi-games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can promote the coexistence of cooperation and punishment, and can reduce the prevalence of defectors under certain parameter regimes. The cost of punishment, the temptation to defect, and the probability of playing the Prisoner's Dilemma versus the Snowdrift game all modulate the long-term fractions of cooperators, punishers, and defectors. The model exhibits multi-stability, oscillatory, and chaotic dynamics, with the possibility of cyclic dominance among strategies. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the dynamical and stationary properties of the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how punishment can affect the prevalence of cooperation and defection in evolutionary games, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. Its results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation or lead to complex dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs, as it is based on two-person games (PD and SD) rather than multi-person public goods games, and the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies rather than payoffs or efficiency.

- source: Horne, C., & Mollborn, S. (2020). Norms: An Integrated Framework. *Annual Review of Sociology, Vol 46*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes sociological theories of norms, focusing on why and how people enforce norms (including through punishment and reward), and the implications for social order and inequality. It distinguishes between consequentialist (cooperation-focused), relational (coordination-focused), and agentic (contextual/individual-focused) approaches. The review highlights that sanctions (punishment and reward) can increase norm compliance, but also that enforcement can reinforce inequalities and sometimes undermine collective welfare, depending on the structure of interests and relationships. The paper does not report new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or group payoff in experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical context for understanding the role of punishment and reward in norm enforcement and social order, but does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games or similar experimental settings. It may inform the interpretation of why punishment can sometimes promote or undermine cooperation, but does not offer parameter-level guidance for the prediction task.

- source: Molho, C., & Wu, J. H. (2021). Direct punishment and indirect reputation-based tactics to intervene against offences. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that both direct punishment and indirect, reputation-based tactics (gossip, ostracism) are important for maintaining cooperation and enforcing norms. Direct punishment is more effective at recalibrating offenders' behavior but is costly and risky, while indirect tactics are less costly and reduce retaliation risk but are less effective at changing behavior. The choice of tactic depends on factors such as the self-relevance of the offense, relationship to the offender, power dynamics, and emotional responses (anger vs. disgust). The paper highlights that indirect tactics are more prevalent in real-world settings than direct punishment as operationalized in laboratory public goods games. It also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce efficiency, and that the ecological validity of lab findings is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers theoretical context suggesting that the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment depend on its cost, the risk of retaliation, and the availability of alternative (indirect) sanctioning mechanisms. It cautions that lab-based punishment games may overestimate the prevalence and impact of direct punishment compared to real-world settings, where indirect tactics are more common. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of communication, relationship context, and the type of punishment available may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, but it does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Phillips, T. (2018). The concepts of asymmetric and symmetric power can help resolve the puzzle of altruistic and cooperative behaviour. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness and function of punishment in promoting cooperation depend critically on the symmetry or asymmetry of power among individuals. In asymmetric power settings (e.g., dominance hierarchies), punishment is typically used by dominants to coerce subordinates, not to enforce reciprocity or group-beneficial cooperation. In symmetric power settings, punishment can be counteracted by retaliation, and cooperation may break down unless specific conditions are met (e.g., territoriality, exit options). The paper reviews evidence that punishment often reduces group payoffs in experimental public goods games, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is crucial for its effectiveness. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments, especially regarding the role of power asymmetries. It suggests that punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency in settings with strong power asymmetries, as it is used for coercion rather than cooperation enforcement. In symmetric power settings, punishment may also fail to improve efficiency due to retaliation and the tragedy of the commons. The paper highlights the importance of considering power relations, cost-to-impact ratios, and the possibility of retaliation when predicting the efficiency effects of punishment. However, it does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in informing the interpretation of empirical results and the design of experiments.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Patil, I., & Cushman, F. (2021). Reputational and cooperative benefits of third-party compensation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that third-party compensation leads to greater reputational and partner choice benefits than third-party punishment, as judged by observers in hypothetical scenarios and vignettes. Compensators are seen as more moral, trustworthy, and are more often chosen as partners in economic games (Trust Game, Dictator Game). These effects are robust across different contexts, including workplace and real-life vignettes. The paper also finds that compensators tend to have lower Dark Triad personality traits. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not provide evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or third-party punishment games. Its findings are relevant for understanding reputational and partner choice consequences of punishment versus compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the hypothetical games, but the dependent variables are not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Thielmann, I., & Hilbig, B. E. (2018). Daring dishonesty: On the role of sanctions for (un)ethical behavior. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that increasing the probability of being checked for dishonesty and increasing the monetary penalty for being caught both substantially reduce the rate of cheating in individual tasks. In Experiment 1, tripling the probability of being checked (from 16.7% to 50%) nearly halved the estimated cheating rate. In Experiment 2, increasing both the probability of being checked and the penalty for being caught further reduced cheating, with the lowest dishonesty observed when both risk and severity were high. The results support utility-based theories of dishonesty, showing that external costs (sanctions) deter unethical behavior. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a direct analog.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the risk and severity of monetary sanctions reduces individual cheating in private reporting tasks. While the findings are highly relevant for understanding the deterrent effect of punishment on norm violations, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. The results may be indirectly informative for predicting that punishment mechanisms with higher detection probability and higher penalty severity are more likely to reduce norm violations, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited by the lack of group interaction, contribution, or payoff aggregation in the study.

- source: Deutchman, P., Bracic, M., Raihani, N., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Punishment is strongly motivated by revenge and weakly motivated by inequity aversion. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment is primarily motivated by revenge (reciprocating losses), but disadvantageous inequity also motivates punishment, even in the absence of loss. The effect of inequity aversion is weaker than the effect of loss. The design manipulates whether the partner can steal from or augment the responder's payoff, and the resulting payoff differences. Punishment is most frequent when the partner steals, especially if this creates disadvantageous inequity. There is also some punishment when the partner does not augment, if this results in disadvantageous inequity. The study does not measure or report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological motivations for punishment in two-player games, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. However, it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used when losses or disadvantageous inequity are present, and that the cost and effectiveness of punishment can moderate its use. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not provide data on group efficiency or welfare with or without punishment.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Hausfeld, J., dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Who initiates punishment, who joins punishment? Disentangling types of third-party punishers by neural traits. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study demonstrates the existence of three distinct types of third-party punishers (independent, conditional, nonpunishers) in a laboratory setting. These types are characterized by different neural traits (gray matter volume in right TPJ, right DLPFC, and bilateral caudate) and by different self-reported motives. The paradigm allows for conditional punishment, where individuals can choose to join punishment only if someone else initiates it. The main findings are about the classification and neural/psychological correlates of these types, not about the impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is informative about individual heterogeneity in punishment behavior and the existence of conditional punishment, which may be relevant for modeling or interpreting punishment dynamics. However, it does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game design dimensions in relation to efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to contextual or mechanistic insight about punishment types.

- source: Rosati, A. G., DiNicola, L. M., & Buckholtz, J. W. (2018). Chimpanzee Cooperation Is Fast and Independent From Self-Control. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees show a fast response bias for prosocial actions (including punishment of theft), but the frequency of such actions is modest and varies across individuals. More prosocial individuals are faster to act in both helping and punishment contexts. There is no evidence that self-control (temporal or motoric) predicts prosocial or punishment behavior. The three measured domains of prosociality (donation, helping, punishment) are uncorrelated, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs, using individual-level donation, helping, and punishment tasks with chimpanzees, but not repeated group games or explicit payoff structures. The main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior exists and is associated with faster response times in chimpanzees, but not with self-control. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, weak support: it suggests that punishment can be a salient behavioral response, but does not inform whether it increases or decreases group efficiency.

- source: Molho, C., Tybur, J. M., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2020). Direct and indirect punishment of norm violations in daily life. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that people frequently use both direct and indirect punishment strategies in response to norm violations in daily life. Direct confrontation is more likely when the punisher has been personally victimized, has more power, or values the offender more. Indirect punishment (gossip, social exclusion) is more likely when the violation is severe, the punisher has less power, or values the offender less. Emotional responses (anger, disgust) also differentially predict punishment type. The findings support the idea that punishment is context-sensitive and motivated by both the benefits of changing others' behavior and the risks of retaliation. However, the study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and contextual determinants of punishment strategy in naturalistic settings, but not for quantifying the impact of punishment on efficiency. The study may be useful for mechanism discussions or for understanding when punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Turpie, J., & Letley, G. (2021). Would community conservation initiatives benefit from external financial oversight? A framed field experiment in Namibia's communal conservancies. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that individuals' willingness to impose social sanctions (disapproval, social exclusion) for norm violations is conditional on their beliefs about the frequency of such violations: the more common a violation is believed to be, the less likely people are to sanction it. This conditionality is strongest for weaker norms (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high regardless of beliefs. The study uses survey data from Austria and applies instrumental variable analysis to establish causality. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the determinants of willingness to sanction norm violations in real-world, non-experimental settings, showing that sanctioning is conditional on perceived prevalence for weaker norms. However, it does not study public goods games or variants directly, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is conceptual: it supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment as a deterrent may depend on beliefs about norm prevalence, which could inform mechanism design or interpretation of experimental results, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: van Dijk, E., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Gross, J. (2020). Power in economic games. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds strong evidence for conditional norm enforcement: individuals' stated willingness to sanction norm violations (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) is lower when they believe such violations are more common. This effect is most pronounced for weaker norms and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high and largely unconditional. The study establishes a causal link between beliefs about norm prevalence and willingness to sanction using an instrumental variables approach. However, all outcomes are based on self-reported intentions in a survey, not on actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its main contribution is to show that willingness to sanction norm violations is conditional on beliefs about others' compliance, especially for weaker norms. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation or efficiency may depend on participants' beliefs about the prevalence of norm violations, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological and social moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Ferguson, E., Quigley, E., Powell, G., Stewart, L., Harrison, F., & Tallentire, H. (2019). To help or punish in the face of unfairness: men and women prefer mutually-beneficial strategies over punishment in a sexual selection context. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three studies using a one-shot 3PPC game, men primed with a romantic context are more likely to choose compensation or both compensation and punishment, rather than punishment alone or doing nothing. Women rate men who compensate (or both compensate and punish) as more attractive, compassionate, fair, and strong, regardless of whether they are considering short-term or long-term relationships. Trait empathy and left-wing political ideology are associated with a preference for compensation. The studies do not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct monetary outcomes for the group; all outcomes are behavioral preferences or social evaluations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. The design is a one-shot third-party punishment and compensation game, and the outcomes are social preferences and evaluations, not efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be relevant for understanding social or reputational motivations for punishment and compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martin, J. W., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., & Cushman, F. (2019). When do we punish people who don't?. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that people are more likely to punish (or endorse punishing) third-party observers who fail to punish norm violators than second-party victims who fail to punish. This pattern is robust across economic games and vignettes, and holds for both severe and non-criminal norm violations. The relationship between the third party and the victim modulates HOP: authorities are punished most for failing to punish, followed by friends, then strangers. The study does not examine or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
