  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its outcomes are exclusively about the social psychology of higher-order punishment—when people punish non-punishers—not about the consequences of punishment for cooperation, contribution, or efficiency. The economic game used is not a public goods game or a close variant, and no group-level payoff or efficiency measures are reported. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., & McCullough, M. E. (2018). The Unresponsive Avenger: More Evidence That Disinterested Third Parties Do Not Punish Altruistically. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across five lab experiments, participants who were directly harmed (insulted) by another person reliably punished the insulter using an aversive sound blast. Participants who witnessed a friend being insulted also punished the insulter, but less strongly. However, participants who witnessed a stranger being insulted generally did not punish the insulter more than they punished uninvolved bystanders, except in one experiment where social censure may have been a factor. The results suggest that third-party punishment is not a robust, general tendency, but is contingent on welfare interdependence (e.g., self or friends). The findings cast doubt on the external validity of economic game results that report high rates of third-party punishment, suggesting those may be inflated by experimental demand. No efficiency or payoff-based outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is to question the generalizability of third-party punishment effects observed in economic games, suggesting that such effects may be overestimated due to experimental demand. For the downstream prediction task, this paper signals that the presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee increased norm enforcement or efficiency, especially when punishment is not personally relevant or when welfare interdependence is low. However, because no efficiency or payoff outcomes are measured, the paper cannot inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Winter, F., & Zhang, N. (2018). Social norm enforcement in ethnically diverse communities. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds strong ethnic asymmetries in social norm enforcement: native Germans are more likely to sanction norm violations, and ethnic minorities are more likely to be the targets of sanctions. These patterns are interpreted as resulting from status inequalities between groups. The aggregate probability of being sanctioned for a norm violation (social control) increases with the share of minorities in the population, but this is driven by natives sanctioning minorities at higher rates. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game or direct variant, but is adjacent in that it studies real-world norm enforcement and punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about real-world punishment (sanctioning) behavior in response to norm violations, with a focus on ethnic asymmetries. It does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. However, it may be useful for understanding how social context (e.g., group identity, status, diversity) can moderate the frequency and direction of punishment, which could be relevant for interpreting or designing PGG experiments with diverse populations. The lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., Shah, Y., Tanaka, H., Ohtsubo, Y., & McCullough, M. E. (2020). When and Why Do Third Parties Punish Outside of the Lab? A Cross-Cultural Recall Study. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in real-life conflicts is infrequent, especially when the third party does not value the welfare of the victim (i.e., low WTR). Intervention (helping the victim without punishing the transgressor) is more common than punishment. The main predictor of both punishment and intervention is the third party's WTR for the victim, with higher WTRs associated with greater likelihood of action. WTR for the transgressor predicts less anger but does not significantly predict intervention or punishment. The results are consistent across U.S. students, U.S. MTurkers, and Japanese students, though Japanese students intervene and punish less often. The study concludes that real-world third-party punishment is much less common than laboratory experiments suggest, likely due to the lack of anonymity, the availability of alternative responses, and the importance of social relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is highly informative about the external validity of laboratory punishment rates: it suggests that real-world third-party punishment is rare, especially among strangers, and that laboratory designs may overstate the prevalence and impact of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the view that punishment's effect on cooperation or efficiency in real-world or naturalistic settings may be much smaller than in lab-based PGGs, particularly when social distance is high and alternative responses are available. The findings highlight the importance of relationship context and available response options as moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Zinchenko, O. (2019). Brain responses to social punishment: a meta-analysis. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that social punishment tasks consistently activate the bilateral claustrum, right inferior frontal gyrus, and left superior frontal gyrus. These regions are associated with salience detection, executive function, and emotional empathy. The meta-analysis includes a range of paradigms (Ultimatum Game, Dictator Game, third-party punishment, social rejection), but does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related economic outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of social punishment but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment on cooperation, efficiency, or payoff in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it confirms that social punishment is a robust neural phenomenon across several economic and social paradigms, it does not report or synthesize any data on group earnings, efficiency, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the impact of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Bar-Gill, O., & Engel, C. (2018). How to Protect Entitlements: An Experiment. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the probability of efficient outcomes (good stays with owner) is high but not perfect, and is affected by the strength of the legal remedy (punishment for taking). Stronger remedies (higher damages or property rule) do not always increase efficiency; in some cases, weaker remedies lead to higher willingness to pay by owners and lower willingness to accept by takers, increasing the chance of a successful deal and thus efficiency. The heterogeneity of fairness norms (positive, negative, flat) and role-dependent beliefs (owners more negative, takers more positive) create information asymmetries that reduce the likelihood of efficient trade. The experiment shows that behavioral factors, not just economic incentives, shape the efficiency of bargaining under different sanction regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment or sanction strength on efficiency is not monotonic: increasing the severity of punishment (stronger legal remedy) does not always increase efficiency and can sometimes reduce it by increasing the gap between parties' willingness to pay and accept. For prediction, this suggests that the relationship between punishment severity and efficiency is mediated by behavioral fairness norms and is not simply positive. The findings are most relevant for settings where efficiency depends on successful bargaining under threat of sanction, and less so for standard public goods games with group punishment. The results caution against assuming that stronger punishment always increases efficiency, especially when fairness norms are heterogeneous.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends. The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Yang, Z. L., Shi, Y. Y., & Li, Y. C. (2018). Analysis of intellectual property cooperation behavior and its simulation under two types of scenarios using evolutionary game theory. *Computers & Industrial Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that under both market and administrative supervision mechanisms, certain policy levers (subsidies, liquidated damages, tax rates, penalties) can increase the probability and stability of cooperation among heterogeneous agents in intellectual property activities. However, the effect is non-monotonic: excessive penalties or taxes can reduce cooperation. The main findings are about the conditions under which the system converges to full cooperation, noncooperation, or mixed states, rather than about efficiency or total group payoff. The results are based on theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for how punishment-like mechanisms (liquidated damages, administrative penalties) and incentives (subsidies, tax breaks) affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured, the results suggest that moderate punishment and incentives can stabilize cooperation, but excessive punishment or taxation can backfire. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design and magnitude of punishment/reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Enke, B. (2019). KINSHIP, COOPERATION, AND THE EVOLUTION OF MORAL SYSTEMS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that kinship structure predicts the type of moral system a society develops: tight kinship societies rely on communal values, shame, revenge, and local institutions, while loose kinship societies develop universal values, guilt, impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment, and large-scale institutions. These systems are internally consistent and mediate cooperation patterns, such as in-group favoritism and trust. Theoretical modeling suggests that universalizing moral systems in loose kinship societies enable more efficient cooperation with strangers, which becomes advantageous with economic development. However, empirical analyses focus on psychological, cultural, and institutional variables, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of punishment and moral systems in supporting cooperation, especially in relation to kinship structure and the scope of cooperation. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments. However, it suggests that impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment is more prevalent in societies with loose kinship ties and universal values, which are theoretically linked to higher efficiency in broad cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how cultural and institutional context may moderate the effectiveness of punishment, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects in controlled game settings.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Gelfand, M., Wu, J. H., Abernathy, J., Akotia, C. S., Aldashev, A., Andersson, P. A., Andrighetto, G., Anum, A., Arikan, G., Aycan, Z., Bagherian, F., Barrera, D., Basnight-Brown, D., Batkeyev, B., Belaus, A., Berezina, E., Björnstjerna, M., Blumen, S., Boski, P., Zeineddine, F. B., Bovina, I., Huyen, B. T. T., Cardenas, J. C., Cekrlija, D., Choi, H. S., Contreras-Ibáñez, C. B., Gill, C. M. H. D., Glöckner, A., Graf, S., Grigoryan, A., Gritskov, V., Growiec, K., Halama, P., Hartanto, A., Hopthrow, T., Hrebícková, M., Ilisko, D., Imada, H., Kapoor, H., Kawakami, K., Khachatryan, N., Kharchenko, N., Khoury, N., Kiyonari, T., Kohút, M., Linh, L. T., Leslie, L. M., Li, Y., Li, N. P., Li, Z., Liik, K., Maitner, A. T., Manhique, B., Manley, H., Medhioub, I., Mentser, S., Mohammed, L., Nejat, P., Nipassa, O., Nussinson, R., Onyedire, N. G., Onyishi, I. E., Özden, S., Panagiotopoulou, P., Perez-Floriano, L. R., Persson, M. S., Pheko, M., Pirttilä-Backman, A. M., Pogosyan, M., Raver, J., Reyna, C., Rodrigues, R. B., Romanò, S., Romero, P. P., Sakki, I., San Martin, A., Sherbaji, S., Shimizu, H., Simpson, B., Szabo, E., Takemura, K., Tieffi, H., Teixeira, M. L. M., Thanomkul, N., Tiliouine, H., Travaglino, G. A., Tsirbas, Y., Wan, R., Widodo, S., Zein, R., Zhang, Q. P., Zirganou-Kazolea, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Perceptions of the appropriate response to norm violation in 57 societies. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that across cultures, the perceived appropriateness of informal sanctions (confrontation, gossip, ostracism) increases as the perceived inappropriateness of a norm violation increases. There is substantial cross-country variation in which sanctions are viewed as most appropriate, and this variation is consistent across norm violation domains (including a common resource depletion scenario). Metanorms for different sanctions are not interchangeable: for example, societies that view physical confrontation as less appropriate tend to view gossip as more appropriate, and vice versa. The perceived appropriateness of sanctions correlates with cultural, economic, and ecological variables (e.g., higher income countries view confrontation as less appropriate and gossip as more appropriate). However, the study does not measure actual cooperation, punishment behavior, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report on actual game play, contributions, or payoffs, nor does it manipulate punishment availability or cost. Its main relevance is contextual: it shows that beliefs about the appropriateness of punishment and other sanctions vary widely across societies, even for cooperation-related norm violations. This may inform expectations about the acceptability or uptake of punishment in different cultural contexts, but does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Bolton, G., Greiner, B., & Ockenfels, A. (2018). Dispute Resolution or Escalation? The Strategic Gaming of Feedback Withdrawal Options in Online Markets. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In laboratory markets with two-sided moral hazard (both buyer and seller can defect), the introduction of a mutual feedback withdrawal option leads to more strategic negative feedback, less honest feedback, and lower efficiency (measured as realized gains from trade divided by maximum possible gains). The feedback system becomes less informative, and trust/trustworthiness are reduced. In one-sided markets (only seller can defect), the feedback withdrawal option does not significantly affect efficiency or the informativeness of feedback. The negative effect of FBW on efficiency is thus conditional on the presence of two-sided moral hazard and mutual withdrawal. The paper also finds that some buyers engage in 'altruistic punishment' by refusing to withdraw negative feedback even at a cost to themselves, partially mitigating the detrimental effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with two-sided moral hazard and mutual punishment/retaliation options, introducing a mutual feedback withdrawal (punishment-like) mechanism reduces efficiency relative to a control without such a mechanism. The effect is not observed in one-sided environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of mutual, bilateral punishment/retaliation options in two-sided games is likely to reduce efficiency, even if the control game is already inefficient. The effect is mediated by strategic retaliation and the breakdown of reputation as an incentive for cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar two-sided, bilateral punishment/retaliation structures.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2021). Free neighborhood choice boosts socially optimal outcomes in stag-hunt coordination problem. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that free neighborhood choice in a repeated stag-hunt game (coordination problem) substantially increases the frequency of efficient coordination and group welfare compared to imposed (fixed) neighborhoods. In the fixed-neighborhood condition, groups mostly converge to the inefficient equilibrium, while in the free-choice condition, efficient outcomes become dominant over time. The ability to exclude inefficient players (a form of endogenous, costly social sanction) is the key mechanism: excluded players are much more likely to switch to efficient behavior, and overall exclusion is infrequent but sufficient to sustain high efficiency. Welfare gains in the free-choice condition are significantly higher, especially in later rounds, despite exclusion being costly. The results suggest that even limited, endogenous social sanctions (via exclusion) can robustly promote efficient group outcomes in coordination settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous, costly exclusion (a form of social sanction) in a repeated coordination game can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when exclusion is rare and costly. While the setting is a stag-hunt (coordination) game rather than a public goods game, the mechanism—costly exclusion of inefficient actors—parallels punishment in PGGs. The findings suggest that, in environments where players can choose their partners and exclusion is possible but costly, efficiency can be much higher than in fixed-group settings. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of endogenous exclusion or partner choice should be expected to have a large positive effect on efficiency, especially over repeated rounds, even if the cost of exclusion is nontrivial. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit punishment stages, as the underlying game is a coordination problem, not a social dilemma.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2021). Is it costly to deceive? People are adept at detecting gossipers' lies but may not reward honesty. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Dishonest gossip reduces efficiency in trust-based interactions: participants who act on dishonest gossip (especially positive lies about untrustworthy agents or negative lies about trustworthy agents) earn lower payoffs than those who act on honest gossip. Participants can detect some dishonest gossip, especially after interacting with the target, and are more likely to trust gossipers they believe to be honest. However, the tendency to socially sanction dishonest gossipers (by reducing trust) is inconsistent and influenced by the valence of the gossip: gossipers who share negative information, even if honest, are trusted less. Thus, while social sanctions for dishonesty exist, they are imperfect and do not always align with actual honesty, limiting their effectiveness in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the presence of dishonest communication in trust-based games reduces efficiency, as measured by participant payoffs. While not a public goods game, the trust game with gossip is adjacent and the mechanism—misleading information undermining cooperation—transfers to PGG-like settings. The findings suggest that, in environments where social sanctions for dishonesty are weak or inconsistently applied, the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms (like gossip) are limited by the prevalence and detectability of dishonesty. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment or sanction mechanisms in improving efficiency depends not only on their presence but also on their alignment with actual dishonest behavior and the social context (e.g., whether negative but honest information is socially penalized). The paper does not manipulate classic PGG punishment dimensions (cost, tech, etc.), but it does show that the ability to sanction dishonesty is necessary but not sufficient for efficiency gains.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Sabin, N., & Reed-Tsochas, F. (2020). Able but Unwilling to Enforce: Cooperative Dilemmas in Group Lending. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group repayment (a direct measure of economic cooperation/efficiency) is highest in groups with moderate social cohesion and no disconnected subgroups. Both very high and very low cohesion are associated with worse repayment. Disconnected subgroups further reduce repayment, and the negative effect is mitigated by higher overall cohesion. The mechanisms are traced to a trade-off between ability and willingness to punish: high cohesion increases ability but reduces willingness to enforce, while disconnected subgroups increase willingness to punish out-group members but reduce ability to do so effectively. The net effect on efficiency is determined by the lesser of ability or willingness. These results are robust to controls for financial ability, group composition, and alternative outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of informal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like settings is highly sensitive to group social structure. In environments where punishment is informal and depends on social ties, both the ability and willingness to punish must be considered. Efficiency is maximized at moderate levels of cohesion; too much cohesion reduces willingness to punish, undermining enforcement. Disconnected subgroups are detrimental to efficiency, even if willingness to punish out-group members is high, because ability is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not monotonic in group connectedness and is context-dependent. The findings are most relevant for settings with informal, endogenous punishment and may not generalize to formal, externally imposed punishment mechanisms.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Local stability of cooperation in a continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity depends on the sensitivity of the assessment and behavioral rules (social norms). It derives explicit conditions (in terms of derivatives of the assessment and behavioral functions) under which a cooperative norm is stable against local mutants, and shows that the benefit-to-cost ratio required for stability increases if reputation is insensitive to observed behavior or if cooperation is insensitive to recipient reputation. The analysis is supported by numerical simulations, which confirm the theoretical predictions about payoff differences between resident and mutant norms. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard PGGs, but reputation loss functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the expected payoff (efficiency) of the group under different norms and parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence about how the structure of social norms (in terms of sensitivity to behavior and reputation) affects the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity. While not a direct PGG or explicit punishment game, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is enforced by reputation-based indirect sanctions rather than direct punishment. The findings suggest that the efficiency of cooperation depends on the responsiveness of the reputation system and the behavioral rule, and that certain parameter regimes (high benefit-to-cost ratio, high sensitivity) are required for stable, efficient cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where indirect reciprocity is the main enforcement mechanism, and less so to direct punishment or reward treatments in PGGs.

- source: Bear, A., & Rand, D. G. (2019). Can Strategic Ignorance Explain the Evolution of Love?. *Topics in Cognitive Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a partner-choice game where both agents can choose to search for better partners (look) or commit (not look), and can allow or disallow their partner to look, the only robust equilibrium is mutual looking and allowing. Commitment (strategic ignorance) does not evolve except when the cost of being abandoned is extremely high. The model directly analyzes the efficiency (expected payoff) of different equilibria and shows that, under most parameterizations, the efficiency of the 'punishment' (commitment-demanding) equilibrium is not higher and is not evolutionarily stable. The findings suggest that, in this class of games, enabling punishment-like mechanisms (demanding commitment) does not increase group efficiency and is not selected for, except in rare, extreme cases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical counterpoint to the idea that punishment or commitment mechanisms generally increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. In the context of partner-choice games with bilateral temptation and the ability to condition on partner behavior, the model predicts that enabling punishment (demanding commitment or non-looking) does not robustly increase efficiency and is not evolutionarily stable. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in games with similar structure (bilateral temptation, partner choice, visible actions), enabling punishment or commitment mechanisms is unlikely to increase efficiency unless there are very large costs to being abandoned. The paper is most informative for adjacent settings rather than standard public goods games, but its payoff-based analysis is directly relevant to efficiency predictions.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Han, T. A., & Tran-Thanh, L. (2018). Cost-effective external interference for promoting the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: This work provides analytical and numerical results showing that an external decision-maker can promote high levels of cooperation in a population of self-interested agents by using a cost-efficient, state-dependent rewarding scheme. Rather than always rewarding cooperators, it is more cost-effective to reward only when the number of cooperators is below a threshold. The optimal threshold depends on the per-individual reward cost, the intensity of selection, and the desired cooperation level. For a wide range of parameters, this targeted approach achieves the same or higher cooperation at a lower total cost than always-on institutional incentives. The model does not directly analyze group payoff or efficiency in the public goods sense, but the results are closely related to maximizing cooperation frequency while minimizing resource expenditure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting the effect of reward-based interventions (not punishment) on cooperation and cost-efficiency in Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. It suggests that targeted, conditional incentives can be more cost-effective than unconditional ones, especially under weak selection. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not analyze punishment, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency with punishment are not possible. The findings support the idea that the design of incentive schemes (e.g., when and whom to reward) strongly moderates the cost-effectiveness of cooperation promotion, and that always-on incentives may be suboptimal. For downstream prediction, the results are most informative for settings with external, state-dependent rewards, and less so for punishment or standard PGGs.

- source: Fulker, Z., Forber, P., Smead, R., & Riedl, C. (2021). Spite is contagious in dynamic networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic networks where agents can choose interaction partners and update their strategies by imitating higher-payoff individuals, spiteful (costly harmful) behavior can reliably invade and fixate in the population. This occurs even when the overall network does not exhibit strong anti-correlation, due to local and transient anti-correlated interactions. The spread of spite is facilitated by reinforcement learning and imitation based on average payoff per interaction. When spiteful behavior dominates, average payoffs are lower than in populations of only 'Social' agents, indicating a reduction in group efficiency. The findings challenge classic conditions for the evolution of costly harm and suggest that dynamic partner choice is a powerful mechanism for the emergence of antisocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not directly study public goods games or experimental punishment interventions. However, it provides indirect evidence that dynamic partner choice and imitation based on payoffs can enable the spread of costly harmful behaviors, which reduce group efficiency. In environments where agents can choose partners and update strategies based on payoffs, the introduction of punishment-like costly harm may decrease efficiency, especially if such behaviors can spread by imitation. The findings suggest caution in predicting positive efficiency effects from punishment in dynamic or endogenous network settings, as costly harm can become self-reinforcing and reduce welfare. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not include a cooperative contribution stage, so direct quantitative prediction is limited, but the qualitative mechanism is relevant for understanding the risks of antisocial punishment or spite in flexible social environments.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, X. P., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2019). Reputation-based adaptive adjustment of link weight among individuals promotes the cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to adaptively increase or decrease based on reputation (interpreted as a form of social reward/punishment) leads to a substantial increase in the frequency of cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. The effect is stronger as the range of possible link weights (controlled by ε) increases, which increases heterogeneity and supports the formation of cooperative clusters. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that dynamic, reputation-based social reward and punishment mechanisms (via link weight adjustment) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a simulation of spatial PDG/SDG rather than a public goods game, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited and should be considered only as suggestive, not directly predictive.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.

  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.

It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.
