  findings: The study finds that introducing environment-based preference selection (parameter \alpha) and environmental heterogeneity (parameter u) into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. The combination of these mechanisms is more effective than either alone. The effect is robust across a range of parameters and network sizes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are thus relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics but not directly for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

  findings: The coevolution of game strategy and link weight in a spatial ultimatum game model leads to higher average proposal and acceptance levels, interpreted as greater fairness. The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect. The model does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction in public goods or similar games is limited.

The 'punishment' in this context is the weakening of social ties, not costly punishment of the type used in PGGs. Therefore, while the findings may be conceptually adjacent—suggesting that dynamic social relationships can promote fairer behavior—they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2021). Local reputation, local selection, and the leading eight norms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that the leading eight social norms for indirect reciprocity can maintain high levels of cooperation in networked populations, even when reputation and strategy updating are local rather than global. However, the ability to sustain cooperation depends on the accuracy of reputation transmission, the speed of evolutionary updating, and the specific norm used. Norms that reward justified defection are less robust to noise and invasion by defectors. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.

Its findings are adjacent: they show that certain reputation-based norms can sustain high cooperation in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks, and that the robustness of cooperation depends on information and evolutionary parameters. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the structure of information and the harshness of social norms can affect the sustainability of cooperation, but it does not quantify efficiency or payoff outcomes. Use this paper for qualitative context about the importance of information structure and norm design, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Danku, Z., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Knowing the past improves cooperation in the future. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in spatial social dilemma games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, stag-hunt) update their strategies based on a combination of current and past payoffs, cooperation is strongly promoted compared to the standard model where only current payoffs matter. This effect is robust to the details of how the past is incorporated (weighted average or single past payoff with decaying probability). The mechanism works by slowing down the invasion of defectors into cooperative clusters, allowing cooperation to persist and spread. The effect is most pronounced when the memory window is sufficiently long or the selected past payoff is not too far in the past. The results are shown for the stationary fraction of cooperators, not for efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or closely related environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are about how memory of past payoffs (a strategy-neutral mechanism) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. While the results may be conceptually relevant for understanding how information and history affect cooperation, they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very weak, indirect context for the role of history or memory in cooperation.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Effects of defensive cooperation strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Li, W. J., Jiang, L. L., & Perc, M. (2021). A limited mobility of minorities facilitates cooperation in social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model, allowing individuals to move away from defectors with low probability (limited mobility) increases the frequency of cooperation by enabling the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters. High mobility, however, undermines cooperation by disrupting network reciprocity. The authors interpret mobility away from defectors as a form of punishment, but the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper claims that limited mobility of minorities can promote cooperation more effectively and with less public resource cost than explicit punishment or reward, but this is not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The analogy between mobility/alienation and punishment is discussed, but there is no explicit manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how indirect forms of sanctioning (like alienation) can promote cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, Q., He, N. R., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Replicator dynamics for public goods game with resource allocation in large populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the coevolutionary dynamics of cooperation and environmental state are more diverse than in well-mixed populations. Depending on payoff parameters and the number of neighbors, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations, bistability (with both depleted and replete environmental states as attractors), coexistence of oscillation and dominance, or stable coexistence of cooperation and defection. The number of neighbors (degree) can either expand or shrink the basin of attraction for favorable environmental states, depending on the ratio of enhancement/degradation rates or payoff parameter ratios. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or model costly punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation and defection in a structured population with environmental feedback, but the 'punishment' parameter is the matrix payoff for mutual defection, not a sanctioning institution. The findings may inform how structural parameters (e.g., number of neighbors) and payoff ratios affect the maintenance of cooperation and environmental quality, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

- source: Pisor, A. C., Gervais, M. M., Purzycki, B. G., & Ross, C. T. (2020). Preferences and constraints: the value of economic games for studying human behaviour. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2021). The complexity of human cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or synthesize results on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, nor does it manipulate punishment or sanctions. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the complexity of social norms and the cognitive costs of implementing strategies can moderate cooperation rates in reputation-based systems. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

- source: Jagers, S. C., Harring, N., Löfgren, Sjöstedt, M., Alpizar, F., Brülde, B., Langlet, D., Nilsson, A., Almroth, B. C., Dupont, S., & Steffen, W. (2020). On the preconditions for large-scale collective action. *Ambio*.

  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that as collective action problems become larger in scale (more actors, greater spatial/temporal distance, more complexity), spontaneous cooperation becomes less likely due to increased stressors such as anonymity, lack of accountability, heterogeneity, and uncertainty. Third-party interventions (which may include sanctions, incentives, or other mechanisms) become increasingly necessary to generate and sustain collective action. The framework is intended to guide future empirical research and policy design, but does not itself provide new empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers theoretical context: it suggests that in larger-scale settings, informal mechanisms (like peer punishment) are less effective, and formal third-party interventions (which may include sanctions or rewards) become more important for sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may diminish as group size, spatial/temporal distance, and complexity increase, unless punishment is institutionalized or externally enforced. The paper does not provide parameter-level guidance for the specific game design dimensions required for quantitative prediction.

- source: Gao, H. Y., Wang, J., Zhang, F., Li, X. P., & Xia, C. Y. (2021). Cooperation dynamics based on reputation in the mixed population with two species of strategists. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based imitation mechanism in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game with both node-type and link-type strategists increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the population is a balanced mix of both types. Link-type strategists can selectively cooperate or defect with neighbors, effectively punishing defectors and protecting cooperative clusters. The strength of the reputation effect and the reputation adjustment factor both positively influence cooperation rates. However, all reported outcomes are in terms of cooperation frequency and network structure, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it models a form of punishment (link-type strategists defecting against defectors) and shows that this increases cooperation rates, it does not report on efficiency, welfare, or group payoff. The model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment mechanism is endogenous to strategy choice rather than an explicit costly punishment option. Thus, the paper may provide some indirect support for the idea that targeted sanctions can promote cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., & Perc, M. (2021). Mathematical foundations of moral preferences. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a wide range of unselfish behaviors in one-shot, anonymous economic games are better explained by a 'moral preference hypothesis'—that is, people have preferences for following their own personal norms about what is right or wrong, beyond the monetary consequences of their actions. This framework organizes and explains empirical deviations from standard social preference models in games such as the dictator game, ultimatum game (altruistic punishment), Prisoner's Dilemma (cooperation), trust game (trustworthiness), sender-receiver game (truth-telling), and trade-off games (equality vs. efficiency). The review also discusses the practical implications of targeting personal norms (rather than social or injunctive norms) in interventions to promote pro-sociality, and proposes a formal utility function incorporating personal norms. However, the paper does not provide new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions in multi-player, repeated, or public goods games. Its main contribution is theoretical: it suggests that personal moral norms are a key driver of unselfish behavior (including punishment) in one-shot, anonymous games, and that interventions targeting personal norms may be more effective than those targeting social norms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic context about why people might punish or cooperate, but not quantitative or design-specific evidence about efficiency effects.

  findings: The paper synthesizes recent advances in the study of cooperation, emphasizing that relatedness (kin selection) is a primary driver of cooperation within species, while enforcement mechanisms (including punishment and rewards) are important for stabilizing cooperation, especially in mutualisms between species. It highlights that enforcement can take the form of sanctions or rewards, but these are discussed as evolutionary strategies rather than as manipulable variables in economic games. The paper also notes that direct fitness benefits and ecological context can influence cooperation, but relatedness is often the most detectable factor. Economic games are mentioned as a tool for studying human cooperation, but the paper is critical of over-interpreting their results for evolutionary theory. No quantitative or experimental results on efficiency or payoff are provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical context for the importance of enforcement mechanisms (punishment, sanctions, rewards) in stabilizing cooperation, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It may inform the general expectation that enforcement can increase cooperation, but it does not specify under what game design conditions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) this effect is positive, negative, or neutral for efficiency. For prediction tasks requiring quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes, this paper is not directly informative.

It reviews evidence that denser networks, higher relational mobility, and certain subsistence or cultural contexts facilitate the spread and impact of reputation, thereby increasing cooperation.   findings: The study finds that cooperation (measured as donations) is higher in small, stable communities and among those with more public religious practice, but not among those with stronger belief in divine punishment. The results suggest that reputation, not fear of supernatural punishment, drives large-scale cooperation in these settings. No evidence is provided on the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

The authors propose that honor norms are a subtype of moral norms, functionally similar in their ability to promote group-beneficial behavior, especially in environments lacking formal enforcement. However, the analysis is conceptual and does not provide empirical evidence or outcome data relevant to efficiency or payoff in public goods or punishment games.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2021). Cooperation in large-scale human societies-What, if anything, makes it unique, and how did it evolve?. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/A | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the key to sustaining cooperation in large-scale societies is the development of new institutional rules (social interaction mechanisms) that make prosocial behavior payoff-compatible for self-interested individuals, rather than a shift to decision-making mechanisms that are insensitive to payoff (e.g., strong conformity or prestige bias). It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that, in both small- and large-scale societies, humans are generally sensitive to material payoffs in social dilemmas, and that institutions such as monitoring, sanctioning, and reputation systems evolve to support cooperation. The paper highlights the importance of distinguishing between cooperative (payoff-increasing for all) and altruistic (payoff-decreasing for the actor) prosocial behaviors, and suggests that in large-scale societies, cooperation among non-relatives is typically cooperative, not altruistic, due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that the effect of punishment and institutional rules on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on whether those rules make cooperation payoff-compatible for self-interested agents. It suggests that, in predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, one should focus on the design of the social interaction mechanism (e.g., the structure and effectiveness of punishment, monitoring, and reward systems) and the degree to which these mechanisms align individual incentives with group welfare. However, the paper does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative relationships between game design dimensions and efficiency outcomes. Its value is in framing the importance of institutional design and payoff-sensitivity, rather than in providing parameter-specific predictions.

- source: de Melo, C. M., Terada, K., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Emotion expressions shape human social norms and reputations. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that participants cooperate more with counterparts who have positive or unknown reputations, use generous strategies, and display cooperative emotion expressions. Reputation, strategy, and emotion all independently and interactively affect cooperation rates and how reputations are updated. Emotion expressions can help forgive defectors with good reputations or rehabilitate those with bad reputations if they behave generously and express cooperative emotions. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses an iterated prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and focuses on cooperation rates and reputation rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the sense of costly punishment; instead, the study manipulates emotion expressions and reputation. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited. It may be useful for understanding how nonverbal cues and reputation affect cooperation, but not for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Vonasch, A. J., Reynolds, T., Winegard, B. M., & Baumeister, R. F. (2018). Death Before Dishonor: Incurring Costs to Protect Moral Reputation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that people value their moral reputation extremely highly, often above wealth, success, or even life itself. In hypothetical scenarios, large proportions of participants preferred jail, amputation, or death to acquiring a bad reputation. In lab experiments, many participants chose to endure pain or disgust (cold pressor, touching worms) rather than have information suggesting they were racist shared with others. The motivation to avoid reputation damage is strong and can drive costly behavior. However, the paper does not use public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a game-theoretic sense, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, support the broader claim that reputation concerns can motivate costly prosocial or norm-compliant behavior, which is a mechanism often invoked in explaining why punishment can sustain cooperation. The findings may be useful for contextualizing why individuals might comply with norms or avoid free-riding, but they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effects of specific game design dimensions. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant and should not be weighted heavily for estimating efficiency outcomes.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The authors propose that human cooperation is uniquely enabled by the evolution of cultural idea systems and kinship-based social identities, which prescribe cooperative behavior and motivate punishment of norm violators as a defense of social identity. They argue that this top-down, culturally mediated mechanism overcomes the second-order free rider problem and stabilizes cooperation in human groups. The paper does not present empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual account of how cooperation and punishment are maintained in human societies.

Its value for the prediction task is limited to high-level theoretical context: it suggests that the presence and strength of culturally mediated identities and norms may be important moderators of cooperation and punishment, but it does not specify how game design dimensions or control efficiency would quantitatively affect efficiency when punishment is enabled. - source: Heath, J., & Rioux, C. (2018). Recent trends in evolutionary ethics: greenbeards!. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that many recent philosophical and scientific models that attempt to explain large-scale human cooperation by introducing commitment or punishment mechanisms (such as strong reciprocity) tacitly rely on greenbeard mechanisms, which are considered implausible in the human case due to their vulnerability to exploitation by 'falsebeards.' The authors argue that these models do not provide a satisfactory solution to the problem of human ultrasociality and that the evolution of cooperation remains an open question. The paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative outcomes relevant to efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance to the prediction task is limited to theoretical critique: it cautions that models relying on punishment to sustain cooperation may be structurally vulnerable unless they avoid greenbeard-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks based on game design dimensions and control efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, high-level theoretical context and does not inform parameter-level predictions.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Park, J. (2018). Changes in political party systems arising from conflict and transfer among political parties. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when both competition (conflict) and transfer (recruitment) rates among three political parties are weak, coexistence of all parties is robust. As either competition or transfer increases, the system transitions to single-party or two-party dominance, with the possibility of bistable or multistable outcomes depending on initial conditions. The maintenance of coexistence is sensitive to the balance between competition and transfer. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not report any payoff or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. Its findings are about the stability of coexistence versus dominance in a theoretical model of party competition and transfer, which is only adjacent to the target domain. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the key game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, MPCR, player count) in a way that maps to efficiency or payoff. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task, though it may provide some very indirect conceptual context about the effects of conflict and transfer on group structure.

- source: Albergaria, M., & Saes, M. S. (2018). Measuring externalities in an information commons: the case of libraries. *Journal of Cleaner Production*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in a library (information commons) setting, users subject to non-monetary sanctions (professors, employees) generate negative externalities for users subject to monetary sanctions (students): an increase in non-student borrowing is associated with a nearly one-to-one decrease in student borrowing. Peer effects are positive but small. The analysis is based on observational data and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding behavioral externalities and the impact of different sanction types in common-pool resource settings, but do not directly inform about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games.

Its findings are limited to behavioral externalities (borrowing counts) in a field setting with different sanction types. While it highlights that sanction type can affect user behavior and externalities, it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the setting is only adjacent to public goods games. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited and indirect.

The study conceptualizes pandemic-related prosocial behaviors as a social dilemma but does not use a public goods game or direct analog, nor does it measure or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. No experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions is present; support for behavioral regulations is measured as an attitude, not as an implemented or experienced sanctioning institution.

While it discusses social dilemmas and support for behavioral regulations (as a proxy for second-order cooperation), it does not manipulate or implement punishment, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The findings are based on self-reported attitudes and behaviors in a real-world pandemic context, not on controlled game-theoretic experiments. Therefore, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited and indirect at best.

- source: Kamei, K. (2021). Incomplete Political Contracts with Secret Ballots: Reciprocity as a Force to Enforce Sustainable Clientelistic Relationships. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Incomplete political contracts (vote buying, promises) are common even with secret ballots and no monitoring, especially when interactions are repeated. Candidates spend significant resources to influence voters, and voters reciprocate, leading to frequent reversals of the election outcome compared to the rational baseline. These reversals almost always reduce group total payoff, i.e., efficiency. Redistribution from candidates to voters reduces within-group inequality but does not increase total welfare. Repetition amplifies these effects: candidates spend more, voters receive more, and outcome reversals are more frequent. The design does not include explicit punishment, but the costly transfers function as a form of strategic expenditure to influence behavior, with negative efficiency consequences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in election-like games with costly, non-enforceable transfers (vote buying, promises), the introduction of such mechanisms reduces group efficiency when they distort the outcome. The effect is more pronounced with repeated interactions. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly, voluntary transfer mechanisms (even without explicit punishment) is likely to reduce efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanisms, especially when repetition is present. The design is not a standard PGG, but the evidence is informative for adjacent settings where costly, strategic transfers are possible. The paper does not study punishment per se, but the efficiency logic is similar: costly, strategic expenditures to influence others can reduce group welfare if they distort optimal outcomes.

- source: Roberts, G. (2020). Honest signaling of cooperative intentions. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Hauert, C., & Walker, R. (2019). Effort Perception is Made More Accurate with More Effort and When Cooperating with Slackers. *Scientific Reports*.

  findings: The study finds that participants are generally accurate in perceiving their partner's effort in a cooperative task, with higher accuracy when the partner is a slacker and when the participant invests more effort. The experimental design involves a two-player online game where both invest effort to move balls into a bucket, with rewards split 50/50. The main outcome is the accuracy of effort perception, not efficiency or payoff. Theoretical modeling suggests that strategies based on effort perception can help avoid inefficient cooperation, but this is not empirically tested in terms of group payoff or efficiency.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding how effort perception may support cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions. The theoretical discussion of effort-prober strategies is adjacent to efficiency but not empirically linked to payoff outcomes.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The study finds that instructing participants to make intuitive decisions (as opposed to reflective or control) increases the amount of reward given to prosocial allocators in a third-party reward task. Honesty-Humility, a personality trait, is not significantly associated with reward behavior. The effect of intuition is robust to controls for other personality dimensions and social value orientation. The study does not examine group efficiency, total payoff, or public goods game settings, and does not include punishment or sanctioning options.

The design is a one-shot third-party reward task based on a dictator game, not a repeated or group-based public goods game. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings may be weakly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of rewarding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the effect of punishment or the efficiency consequences of institutional design in public goods games.

- source: Bradley, A., Lawrence, C., & Ferguson, E. (2018). Does observability affect prosociality?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that making prosocial actions observable increases prosocial behavior (r = 0.141), with stronger effects in repeated interactions, social dilemmas (including public goods games), and when decisions have consequences for the actor. The effect is also stronger in laboratory settings and with passive observers. However, the outcome is always a behavioral measure (e.g., contribution, donation, volunteering) or intention, not efficiency or group payoff. The analysis does not directly address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observability (e.g., making contributions public) can increase prosocial behavior in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in repeated games and social dilemmas. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or the effect of punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that increasing observability may increase contributions, which could in turn affect efficiency, but without direct evidence. It does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report the relevant game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct mapping to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Blair, G., Littman, R., & Paluck, E. L. (2019). Motivating the adoption of new community-minded behaviors: An empirical test in Nigeria. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study found that both a social norms intervention (film with role models) and a nudge intervention (mass text message) increased the number of people who reported corruption by text message in Nigerian communities. The mass text message had a much larger and more immediate effect than the film. The film shifted some perceptions of social norms (e.g., increased perceived prevalence of corruption and anger about corruption), but did not increase the perceived prevalence of reporting. There was no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and no punishment or sanction mechanism was present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental context is adjacent to public goods (encouraging costly community-minded behavior), but there is no group payoff, no efficiency measure, and no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding the adoption of rare cooperative behaviors, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: van Leeuwen, B., Schram, A., & Offerman, T. (2020). COMPETITION FOR STATUS CREATES SUPERSTARS: AN EXPERIMENT ON PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION AND NETWORK FORMATION. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that competition for status (via status rents for incoming links) is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient public good provision in repeated network games. When status rents are high, groups tend to form stable star or superstar networks, with a single core player providing the public good at or above the efficient level, and periphery players free-riding. Efficiency (measured as group earnings or welfare relative to the efficient benchmark) is much higher with status rents, especially in larger groups. In contrast, without status rents, groups fail to coordinate on efficient outcomes, and efficiency is low. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It is not suitable for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as punishment is not present in any treatment. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how other mechanisms (status rents, endogenous network formation, group size) affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms that can increase efficiency in repeated public goods environments.

- source: Johnson, T., & Smirnov, O. (2018). Inequality as information: Wealth homophily facilitates the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed population playing repeated one-shot prisoners' dilemma games, a strategy that uses wealth homophily (COEQUALS) can successfully invade and dominate a population of defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs (efficiency). The success of COEQUALS is robust to noise, population size, number of rounds, and the presence of certain mimics. The key moderator is the ability to observe the wealth of a sufficient portion of the population: when COEQUALS agents can view at least 10-20% of the population's wealth, the strategy spreads widely and average payoffs increase. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings suggest that visible inequality can serve as an informational mechanism to support cooperation and efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma environments where agents can condition their cooperation on visible wealth (past payoffs), but it does not address punishment or sanctions. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling positive assortment (e.g., wealth homophily) can substitute for punishment in supporting high efficiency. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is less directly relevant, but it does highlight the importance of informational cues and partner selection. The results also indicate that efficiency is robust to variations in player count, number of rounds, and noise, but is sensitive to the ability to observe others' payoffs. If a public goods game allows for similar informational mechanisms, efficiency may be high even without punishment.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2019). Nonconformity of cooperators promotes the emergence of pure altruism in tag-based multi-agent networked systems. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in tag-based multi-agent systems, the combination of spatial structure (small-world networks) and the presence of nonconforming (free) altruists, together with conformity-biased transmission of other strategies, is necessary for the stable dominance of pure altruism (unconditional cooperation). Conformity alone, or spatial structure alone, is insufficient to produce this outcome. The model shows that even a small fraction (>1%) of nonconforming altruists can drive the system toward near-complete altruism, resulting in higher mean fitness and group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust across a range of network parameters and conversion sensitivities. The results are supported by both agent-based simulations and mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about the role of conformity, spatial structure, and unconditional cooperation in evolutionary games with tag-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does provide a strong theoretical and computational baseline for understanding how other mechanisms (conformity, spatial structure, nonconformity) can promote efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment. If a model or prediction task involves tag-based cooperation, conformity, or spatial structure, the findings here suggest that these factors can be powerful moderators of efficiency, but the effect of punishment would need to be assessed from other sources.

- source: Li, C., Xu, H. D., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Synergistic effects of self-optimization and imitation rules on the evolution of cooperation in the investor sharing game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the proposed dynamic strategy updating rule can substantially increase the density of cooperators in the investor sharing game, especially at low aspiration levels. The self-optimization rule increases the probability that defectors with high investment become cooperators, which then promotes further imitation of cooperation. Lower market monopoly (parameter alpha) and more random network structure also boost cooperation, but the effect of network randomness is stronger. Average payoffs for cooperators can exceed those for defectors at high cooperation rates, but group efficiency is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.

Its findings on cooperation rates and average payoffs under different strategy updating rules and aspiration levels may be of indirect interest for understanding how endogenous learning rules affect cooperation, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report group efficiency as defined for the prediction task.

- source: Yang, C. L., & Yue, C. S. J. (2019). Cooperation in an Assortative Matching Prisoners Dilemma Experiment with Pro-Social Dummies. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that assortative matching based on recent cooperation history can increase cooperation rates compared to random matching, but the effect is inconsistent across groups. Introducing pro-social dummies (always-cooperating computer players) into the assortative matching environment (WHc) leads to a substantial and robust increase in cooperation rates for all types of players, including those initially inclined to defect. The presence of dummies shifts the distribution of cooperation rates to higher values and reduces the prevalence of all-defection. Payoff is positively correlated with cooperation in the assortative matching treatments, but efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured or reported. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of assortative matching and exogenous pro-social agents on cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does show that structural features (like matching rules and initial population composition) can have large effects on cooperation rates, which may be conceptually relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Liu, Y. F., Geng, Y. N., Du, C. P., Hu, K. P., Shen, C., Pansini, R., & Shi, L. (2021). The interface of unidirectional rewards: Enhanced cooperation within interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that unidirectional, asymmetric reward from an upper to a lower network layer can sustain or enhance cooperation in both layers, depending on the temptation to defect and the reward factor. When only pro-social reward is allowed, defectors tend to dominate; when only anti-social reward is allowed, cooperators can dominate. The model does not include punishment or negative sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The results are based on simulations and theoretical analysis of cooperation rates, not on empirical or experimental data.

The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) affect cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is adjacent to, but not the same as, a public goods game, and the outcome measures are not payoff-based.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Rule Following Mitigates Collaborative Cheating and Facilitates the Spreading of Honesty Within Groups. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals who are more likely to follow arbitrary, costly rules are also more likely to behave honestly in situations where cheating is possible and profitable. In dyadic and four-person group die-rolling tasks, groups with more high rule-followers exhibit less collaborative cheating (i.e., lower rates of reporting doubles and lower average reported die-rolls). The presence of even a single high rule-follower in a group can reduce collaborative cheating among low rule-followers. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or any formal enforcement mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes beyond the behavioral proxies for dishonesty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related enforcement mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. Its findings are relevant to understanding the role of individual differences in rule-following and the dynamics of collaborative cheating in the absence of punishment, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Du, J., Thomas, M. G., Bårdsen, B. J., Mace, R., & Næss, M. W. (2019). Comparison of social complexity in two independent pastoralist societies. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some contextual insight into how cooperation is structured in real-world groups, but not in a way that informs the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, populations where all players are overconfident or underconfident about their own payoffs can sustain higher levels of cooperation than populations with unbiased self-perception. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate levels of bias and is robust across different network structures, provided the population is structured (not well-mixed). The mechanism is attributed to changes in the rates and asymmetries of strategy invasion, which favor the persistence of cooperation. However, the paper does not include punishment, reward, or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is adjacent to the prediction task because it analyzes cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with altered imitation dynamics (via self-perception bias), but does not manipulate or discuss punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how changes in imitation or perception dynamics can affect cooperation, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

  findings: The model demonstrates that the coevolution of language and cooperation can allow cooperators to form a common language, recognize each other, and selectively cooperate, thereby maintaining high levels of cooperation even in the presence of defectors. Network structure (spatial structure) further facilitates the emergence and maintenance of both cooperation and a common language. The model explores how various parameters (population size, communication rounds, learning noise, etc.) affect the fraction of cooperators and language consistency. However, the model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report on efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.

- source: Tan, S. L. (2019). Proximity inheritance explains the evolution of cooperation under natural selection and mutation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that proximity inheritance in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model leads to the emergence and maintenance of high levels of cooperation, even in the presence of mutation. Cooperation is favored when offspring are placed within the neighborhood of their parents, and the effect is robust to variations in temptation of defection, selection intensity, and mutation rate. The model demonstrates that dynamic clustering of cooperators can arise, and that cooperation collapses if proximity inheritance is removed (i.e., if offspring are placed randomly or at the site of the dead individual). However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how spatial structure and inheritance of proximity can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in reproductive ability—where a player's likelihood of updating their strategy depends on their own and their neighbors' payoffs—can promote higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. There is an optimal intermediate level of heterogeneity that maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much heterogeneity is less effective. The results are based on simulations measuring the fraction of cooperators over time and under different parameter settings. No punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/welfare outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings about heterogeneity in updating rules may be of indirect conceptual interest for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that making network edge weights depend on recent cooperation (reputation) strongly increases the fraction of cooperators in the population, compared to a standard unweighted network. The effect is strongest when only the most recent action is used for reputation (short memory). Longer memory reduces the benefit for cooperation. The mechanism is that cooperators in clusters gain higher edge weights and thus higher payoffs, helping them resist defectors. However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. Its findings about reputation-based network weighting may be relevant for understanding how endogenous payoff structures can promote cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. - source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.

It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
