Its findings are only weakly relevant for the prediction task, as the mechanism studied (reputation-based teaching ability) is distinct from punishment, and the outcomes are limited to cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff. The game structure is also not a public goods game but rather spatial PDG and SDG. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Zhang, M., Wang, S. Y., Hu, X. T., & Alfaro-Bittner, K. (2021). Local and global information affect cooperation in networked Prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with global information about their opponents' strategies, or allowing them to choose between local and global information, increases the frequency of cooperation compared to providing only local information. In the local information condition, cooperation declines steadily over time. In the global and discretionary conditions, some sessions show a reversal with increasing cooperation in later rounds. Players strongly prefer global information when given a choice. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a networked Prisoner's Dilemma without punishment or reward. Its main relevance is indirect: it suggests that the type and scope of information available to players can influence cooperation rates, which may in turn affect efficiency in related games. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper offers only weak, contextual support for the prediction task.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, L. P., & He, M. F. (2020). Social dilemma based on reputation and successive behavior. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that increasing the emphasis on reputation (parameter beta) promotes higher cooperation rates, even under higher temptation to defect. Two reputation formation mechanisms are compared: one rewards consecutive cooperation, the other rewards switching from defection to cooperation. The effectiveness of each depends on the size of the reputation reward and the emphasis on reputation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome. The findings are about behavioral cooperation, not economic efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Its results on reputation and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding indirect reciprocity, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Chen, W. M. (2020). Effects of attitudes on the evolution of cooperation on complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a networked weak prisoner's dilemma, populations with more positive average attitudes achieve higher average cooperation levels, while negative attitudes reduce cooperation. Attitude heterogeneity decreases cooperation when the average attitude is positive, but increases cooperation when the average attitude is negative. These effects are consistent across both regular (square lattice) and scale-free (BASF) networks. The model does not include punishment, rewards, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, and focuses on behavioral cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency outcomes. Its findings about attitudes and subjective utility may be contextually relevant for understanding behavioral drivers of cooperation, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency. The model is adjacent to PGGs (as a weak prisoner's dilemma on networks), but lacks the key design dimensions and outcome types required for the prediction task.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Zhang, Z. P., Wang, X. Y., Yan, M., Zhang, Q. F., & Zhang, S. H. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in the multigame on a two-layer square network. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that introducing multigame environments (where agents probabilistically play different two-player social dilemmas) and coupling between network layers can enhance the fraction of cooperators in the population. The effect of coupling strength and game heterogeneity is explored, as well as the impact of uncertainty in strategy updating. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff. The findings are limited to cooperation rates and their dependence on model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rates in multigame, multilayer network settings may be of limited adjacent interest for understanding the evolution of cooperation, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2019). Collective Action for War and for Peace: A Case Study among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that collective action for war is often easier to mobilize than for peace, due to emotional triggers, private benefits for some individuals (especially young men), and the contagiousness of anger and revenge. Peacemaking, by contrast, requires overcoming internal rifts, mistrust, and the lack of unifying emotions, but is facilitated by strong leadership, compensation payments, and the involvement of broader social networks (including women and affinal kin). The development of rules and institutions for peacemaking (e.g., compensation, mediation) reduced the costs and destructiveness of war, promoted social complexity, and shifted incentives toward peace. However, the breakdown of traditional norms (e.g., with the introduction of guns) led to more destructive wars and greater reluctance to participate. Sanctions against free riders in war are rare due to the importance of brotherhood, but in peacemaking, non-contributors can be gradually reintegrated. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in a formal sense, but describes the social and material costs and benefits of war and peace.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It offers contextual and theoretical insights into how collective action, norm enforcement, and compensation (a form of sanction) operate in a real-world society, and how these mechanisms affect the costs and benefits of group action. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in a way that can be mapped to PGG outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in illustrating the complexity of sanctioning, the importance of leadership and social structure, and the potential for compensation to restore balance after conflict. It should be used as contextual background rather than as direct input for quantitative prediction.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Aksoy, O. (2021). Does Herding Undermine the Trust Enhancing Effect of Reputation? An Empirical Investigation with Online-Auction Data. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: Using a large dataset of eBay auctions, the study shows that sellers with more positive reputations are more likely to sell their items and at higher prices, while negative reputations reduce these outcomes. Buyers exhibit herding behavior, being more likely to bid on items that already have bids, but this effect peaks and then declines as the number of bidders increases. Importantly, even when herding, buyers still consider seller reputation, and the effect of reputation on sales and price remains significant. There is no evidence that herding undermines the efficiency or trust-enhancing effect of reputation systems in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation mechanisms (a form of informal sanctioning) increase efficiency in online market environments by raising both the probability of sale and the price sellers can obtain. While not a public goods game or direct punishment setting, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of reputation-based sanctions on efficiency in environments where trust and cooperation are important. The results suggest that, even in the presence of herding (social influence), reputation systems continue to enhance efficiency. However, the market context differs from standard PGGs, so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Falk, A., Becker, A., Dohmen, T., Enke, B., Huffman, D., & Sunde, U. (2018). GLOBAL EVIDENCE ON ECONOMIC PREFERENCES. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds substantial heterogeneity in economic preferences across and within countries. Preferences such as patience, risk tolerance, altruism, reciprocity, and trust are systematically related to individual characteristics (age, gender, cognitive ability) and to country-level geographic and cultural variables. Patience is strongly correlated with economic development (GDP per capita), while negative reciprocity is correlated with the frequency of armed conflicts. Social preferences (altruism, positive reciprocity, trust) are associated with prosocial behaviors and charitable activities. However, the study does not involve public goods games, group efficiency, or experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it measures negative reciprocity (willingness to punish unfairness) as a preference and relates it to macro-level outcomes (e.g., conflict), it does not analyze the effect of punishment opportunities on group payoff, efficiency, or cooperation in controlled game settings. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very indirect context on the prevalence of punishment motives across societies.

- source: Peperkoorn, L. S., Becker, D. V., Balliet, D., Columbus, S., Molho, C., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2020). The prevalence of dyads in social life. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Across five studies (four surveys and one experience-sampling), dyads are consistently the most common group size reported for a variety of social activities in both the US and the Netherlands. The paper finds some small sex differences (women are sometimes more dyadic), and minor associations with age and relationship status, but the main result is the predominance of dyads. The discussion connects this to theories of reciprocity, coordination, and social exclusion, but does not empirically address cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report on any economic game, nor does it analyze efficiency, payoff, or punishment. Its only indirect relevance is in highlighting that most real-world social interactions occur in dyads, which may inform the ecological validity of two-player game studies versus larger group games. However, it does not provide any data or analysis that would inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kouchaki, M. (2021). Virtuous victims. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people perceive victims of immorality as more moral and trustworthy than nonvictims, even when their behavior is identical. This effect is specific to moral traits and does not extend to nonmoral traits or to victims of accidents. The effect is explained by the Justice Restoration Hypothesis: seeing victims as virtuous motivates people to help them and punish perpetrators. The effect disappears when there are disincentives for justice-restorative action. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, but does show that perceived victim virtue increases willingness to punish and help in hypothetical contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or measure group payoff, efficiency, or actual economic outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that willingness to punish is motivated by perceptions of victim virtue, and that social incentives can modulate this willingness. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually relevant for understanding psychological motivations behind punishment and justice-restorative actions.

- source: Galeotti, F., Maggian, V., & Villeval, M. C. (2021). FRAUD DETERRENCE INSTITUTIONS REDUCE INTRINSIC HONESTY. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Exposure to centralized deterrence institutions (ticket inspections and sanctions) increases subsequent unethical behavior (dishonesty) in a different context, for both previous rule-breakers and compliers. The effect is robust to controls and is stronger with larger inspection teams, suggesting a possible normative or signaling mechanism. The study does not use a public goods game or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about behavioral spillovers of punishment institutions, not about their effect on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not manipulate or report on any of the key PGG design dimensions. Its findings are about behavioral spillovers of deterrence institutions on individual honesty in unrelated contexts. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may be of conceptual interest for understanding broader effects of punishment institutions.

- source: Neilands, P., Hassall, R., Derks, F., Bastos, A. P. M., & Taylor, A. H. (2020). Watching eyes do not stop dogs stealing food: evidence against a general risk-aversion hypothesis for the watching-eye effect. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Dogs are sensitive to owner commands and are slower to take forbidden food when told to 'leave' compared to 'go.' However, the presence of images of eyes does not further increase their latency to steal food compared to control images. This suggests that the watching-eye effect, robustly observed in humans, does not generalize to dogs, even though dogs are highly sensitive to human eye cues in other contexts. The findings argue against a general risk-aversion explanation for the watching-eye effect and support the idea that it is linked to human-specific reputation management. No efficiency, payoff, or group-level outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. The study is about animal behavior (dogs) in a food-stealing paradigm, not about human cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games. While the discussion references punishment and reputation management in humans, these are not empirically tested in this study. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Loch-Temzelides, T. (2021). Walrasian equilibrium behavior in nature. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the trading behavior of mycorrhizal fungi in exchanging phosphorus for carbon with plant roots is consistent with Walrasian equilibrium, as revealed by the satisfaction of GARP in the observed data. This implies that the resulting allocations are Pareto efficient. The study provides a proof-of-concept that biological markets can achieve efficient outcomes as predicted by general equilibrium theory, even in the absence of human participants or explicit institutional mechanisms. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and revealed preference tests using data from a prior biological experiment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study public goods, punishment, or sanctions. Its findings are relevant to the efficiency of biological market exchanges and the applicability of Walrasian equilibrium, but not to the design dimensions or efficiency outcomes of PGGs with or without punishment. It provides no evidence on how punishment, player count, or other PGG design features affect efficiency.

- source: Mujcic, R., & Leibbrandt, A. (2018). Indirect Reciprocity and Prosocial Behaviour: Evidence from a Natural Field Experiment. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The study provides strong field evidence that upstream indirect reciprocity substantially increases prosocial behavior in one-shot interactions among strangers. Drivers who were helped by another driver were 32.1% likely to help a third party, compared to 14.6% in the baseline (a 119% increase). This effect is robust to gender, age, social class, presence of co-passengers, traffic congestion, and the physical distance (time elapsed) since being helped. The authors discuss that such increased prosociality could reduce waiting times and improve social welfare, especially during busy periods, but do not directly measure efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study public goods games or punishment, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on indirect reciprocity and prosocial behavior are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that certain social mechanisms (here, being helped) can increase prosocial actions in one-shot field settings. However, the design, context, and outcome measures are not directly transferable to public goods games with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the behavioral drivers of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kardas, M., Shaw, A., & Caruso, E. M. (2018). How to Give Away Your Cake and Eat It Too: Relinquishing Control Prompts Reciprocal Generosity. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that when one person abdicates a resource allocation decision to another, the recipient is more likely to respond generously (give the better resource to the abdicator) than in a baseline where no abdication occurs. This effect is robust across hypothetical and real-stakes settings, and is mediated in part by perceptions of the abdicator's generosity. The studies are all dyadic, with two individuals and a binary allocation (better/worse resource), and do not involve repeated interaction, group cooperation, or any form of punishment or sanction. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design, outcomes, and mechanisms studied (abdication, reciprocity, perceived generosity) are not related to punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes in group cooperation games. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game-like settings.

- source: Capraro, V., Perc, M., & Vilone, D. (2020). Lying on networks: The role of structure and topology in promoting honesty. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that network structure has a nontrivial effect on the evolution of honesty and lying in the sender-receiver game. For black lies and altruistic white lies, honesty is more likely to evolve in small-world networks and, to a lesser extent, in the one-dimensional ring, compared to well-mixed populations. For Pareto white lies, lying is more likely to evolve in small-world networks. For spiteful lies, honesty always prevails regardless of network. The outcomes are reported in terms of the densities of liars and believers, not in terms of payoffs or efficiency. The sender-receiver game is fundamentally different from public goods games and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on network structure and the evolution of honesty in the sender-receiver game are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGGs.

- source: Sutter, M., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Lergetporer, P. (2018). Language group differences in time preferences: Evidence from primary school children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that German-speaking children are significantly more patient (more likely to delay gratification) than Italian-speaking children, even after controlling for age, IQ, risk attitudes, and family background. This difference is robust across different elicitation methods and is also reflected in self-reported saving behavior. Children from bilingual households show intermediate levels of patience. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not study group cooperation, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on time preferences and cultural/language group differences in patience are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public goods games.
